 c:=\sum_{i=1}^m z_i z_i^*\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}_B(h).$$ For the containment, use $z_i z_i^*\le\|g\|^2v_i v_i^*$ and heredity. Additivity and traciality give the asserted identity for every extended trace, with the same $c$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-ext-lem-rounding"></a>
<div id="ext:lem:rounding" class="lemma">

<a id="ext:eq:rounding"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Integer spectral rounding). *Let $B$ be a stable $C^*$-algebra, $c\in B_+$, and let $L$ be an integer greater than $\|c\|$. Put $$w=\sum_{i=1}^L[(c-i)_+]\in\mathrm{Cu}(B).$$ For every lower-semicontinuous extended trace $\tau$, writing $\varphi=d_\tau$, one has $$\label{ext:eq:rounding}
 \varphi(w)\le\tau(c)\le\varphi(w)+d_\tau(c).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ext-lem-rounding"><summary>Proof of stmt-ext-lem-rounding</summary>

*Proof.* For $q(t)=\min(t,1)$ on $[0,\infty)$, scalar functional calculus gives the exact identity <a id="ext:eq:staircase"></a>$$\label{ext:eq:staircase}
 c=\sum_{i=1}^L q((c-(i-1))_+).$$ Set $R=\max(1,\|c\|)$. For every integer $m\ge1$, $$\big((c-i)_+/R\big)^{1/m}\le q((c-(i-1))_+).$$ Indeed, below the spectral threshold $i$ the left side is zero, and above it the right side is one. Consequently, $$d_\tau((c-i)_+)
 \le\tau(q((c-(i-1))_+))
 \le d_\tau((c-(i-1))_+).$$ The last inequality uses Equation <a href="#eq:pre:trace-rank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:trace-rank">[eq:pre:trace-rank]</a> and hereditary containment. Summing and using $(c-L)_+=0$ proves Equation <a href="#ext:eq:rounding" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:rounding">[ext:eq:rounding]</a>. Only the first rank $d_\tau(c)$ remains as error; the number $L$ of spectral levels does not occur in the error bound. ◻

</details>

We now combine the two lemmas. If $M[h]\le[e]$ and $c$ is obtained by trace transfer into $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(h)$, then $M[c]\le[e]$. For a functional with $\varphi([e])<\infty$, rounding therefore approximates $\tau(r(a))$ to within $\varphi([e])/M$. A single cut-supported function does not recover the full rank of an arbitrary element. In the next section we write that rank as a sum of such trace values and choose the support ranks so that the errors are summable. Neither the norm of $c$ nor the number of its spectral levels has to be bounded uniformly.

<a id="ext:sec:divisibility"></a>
### Almost divisibility with a relative error

For a given class $x$ and integer $N\ge1$, we seek a single class $u$ with $\varphi(x)/(N+1)<\varphi(u)<\varphi(x)/N$ whenever $0<\varphi(x)<\infty$. We choose $s$ between $1/(N+1)$ and $1/N$ and approximate $s\varphi(x)$ from below. The error is measured by one fixed cut, so the same construction also controls functionals taking the value infinity on $x$.

<a id="stmt-ext-thm-divisibility"></a>
<div id="ext:thm:divisibility" class="theorem">

**Theorem 11**. *Let $A$ be a simple exact non-elementary $C^*$-algebra with strict comparison as in Equation <a href="#ext:eq:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:comparison">[ext:eq:comparison]</a>. For every $x\in\mathrm{Cu}(A)$ and integer $N\ge1$, there exists $u\in\mathrm{Cu}(A)$ such that $Nu\le x\le(N+1)u$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ext-thm-divisibility"><summary>Proof of stmt-ext-thm-divisibility</summary>

*Proof.* The assertion is immediate for $x=0$. Otherwise write $x=[a]$, where $a\in B_+$ has norm one, and put $e=(a-1/2)_+$. If $\mathrm F_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)$ is empty, all functionals are zero or infinite on every nonzero class. Equation <a href="#ext:eq:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:comparison">[ext:eq:comparison]</a> then gives $Nx\le x$, while $x\le(N+1)x$ is automatic. Take $u=x$. We henceforth assume $\mathrm F_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)\ne\varnothing$.

<a id="ext:eq:budget"></a>
*Choose supports with summable relative rank.* Choose a real number $s$ and integers $M_j\ge1$, $j\ge1$, such that $$\label{ext:eq:budget}
 \frac1{N+1}<s<\frac1N,\qquad
 E:=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac1{M_j}<s-\frac1{N+1}.$$ Lemma <a href="#ext:lem:small" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:lem:small">7</a> supplies nonzero $h_j\in B_+$ with $M_j[h_j]\le[e]$.

<a id="ext:eq:transferred"></a>
To recover the full rank of $a$ from functions vanishing near zero, choose increasing cutoffs $F_j$ converging to one on $(0,1]$ and transfer their positive increments. Concretely, set $$F_0=0,\qquad F_j(t)=\min\{1,\max\{0,2^jt-1\}\},\qquad
 r_j=s(F_j-F_{j-1})\quad(j\ge1).$$ The functions $F_j$ increase, and $r_j\ge0$ vanishes on $[0,2^{-j}]$. Put $p_j=r_j(a)$. Lemma <a href="#ext:lem:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:lem:transfer">9</a> gives $c_j\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(h_j)_+$ satisfying $$\label{ext:eq:transferred}
 \tau(c_j)=\tau(p_j)\quad\text{for all extended traces }\tau,
 \qquad M_j[c_j]\le[e].$$

<a id="ext:eq:u-values"></a>
*Round and sum inside the Cuntz semigroup.* Choose integers $L_j>\|c_j\|$ and put <a id="ext:eq:u"></a>$$\label{ext:eq:u}
 w_j=\sum_{i=1}^{L_j}[(c_j-i)_+],\qquad
 u=\sup_{k\ge1}\sum_{j=1}^k w_j.$$ For completeness, this supremum is represented by a positive element of $B$: choose representatives of $w_j$, rescale them to norm at most $2^{-j}$, and put them in mutually orthogonal stable blocks. Their sum converges in norm. Each finite partial sum is Cuntz below it. Conversely, comparison of all partial sums with one target passes to the norm limit, proving the least-upper-bound property. Therefore $$\label{ext:eq:u-values}
 \varphi(u)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\varphi(w_j)
 \quad(\varphi\in\mathrm F(\mathrm{Cu}(A))).$$

<a id="ext:eq:piece-error"></a>
Fix $\varphi\in\mathrm F_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)$ and choose its representing trace using Equation <a href="#ext:eq:trace-representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:trace-representation">[ext:eq:trace-representation]</a>. Equations <a href="#ext:eq:rounding" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:rounding">[ext:eq:rounding]</a> and <a href="#ext:eq:transferred" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:transferred">[ext:eq:transferred]</a> give $$\label{ext:eq:piece-error}
 \varphi(w_j)\le\tau(p_j)
 \le\varphi(w_j)+\frac{\varphi([e])}{M_j}.$$ The finite value $\varphi([e])$ is the only trace-dependent constant in the error estimate.

<a id="ext:eq:recover"></a>
*Recover the rank of $a$.* Telescoping gives $$\sum_{j=1}^k\tau(p_j)=s\tau(F_k(a)).$$ We claim that $$\label{ext:eq:recover}
 \tau(F_k(a))\uparrow d_\tau(a)=\varphi(x).$$ The values increase and are at most $d_\tau(a)$ by Equation <a href="#eq:pre:trace-rank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:trace-rank">[eq:pre:trace-rank]</a> and hereditary containment. Given $0<\epsilon<1$, for sufficiently large $k$ we have $F_k(t)=1$ on $[\epsilon,1]$. Thus, simultaneously for all integers $m\ge1$, $$(a-\epsilon)_+^{1/m}\le F_k(a).$$ Taking traces and then letting $m\to\infty$ gives $d_\tau((a-\epsilon)_+)\le\tau(F_k(a))$. The cut-supremum formula in Equation <a href="#ext:eq:cu-facts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:cu-facts">[ext:eq:cu-facts]</a> now proves Equation <a href="#ext:eq:recover" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:recover">[ext:eq:recover]</a>.

<a id="ext:eq:total-error"></a>
<a id="ext:eq:strict-interval"></a>
Sum Equation <a href="#ext:eq:piece-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:piece-error">[ext:eq:piece-error]</a>, then use Equations <a href="#ext:eq:u-values" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:u-values">[ext:eq:u-values]</a> and <a href="#ext:eq:recover" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:recover">[ext:eq:recover]</a>. In extended nonnegative arithmetic, for every $\varphi\in\mathrm F_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)$, $$\label{ext:eq:total-error}
 \varphi(u)\le s\varphi(x)
 \le\varphi(u)+E\varphi([e]).$$ If $\varphi(x)<\infty$, Equation <a href="#ext:eq:controller" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:controller">[ext:eq:controller]</a> implies $$(s-E)\varphi(x)\le\varphi(u)\le s\varphi(x).$$ Since $\varphi(x)>0$, Equation <a href="#ext:eq:budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:budget">[ext:eq:budget]</a> yields $$\label{ext:eq:strict-interval}
 \frac{\varphi(x)}{N+1}<\varphi(u)<\frac{\varphi(x)}N.$$ If instead $\varphi(x)=\infty$, then $E\varphi([e])<\infty$ and Equation <a href="#ext:eq:total-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:total-error">[ext:eq:total-error]</a> forces $\varphi(u)=\infty$. No subtraction of infinite values is used. Since $\mathrm F_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)$ is nonempty, these conclusions also prove $u\ne0$.

*Apply comparison with each target.* Zero functionals vanish at both $x$ and $u$, and the everywhere-infinite functionals are infinite at both. For functionals in $\mathrm F_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)$, the preceding estimates give $$\varphi(Nu)\le\varphi(x),\qquad
 \varphi(x)\le\varphi((N+1)u).$$ The first inequality is strict whenever $0<\varphi(x)<\infty$. For the second, if $0<\varphi((N+1)u)<\infty$, then the functional belongs to $\mathrm F_{\mathrm{fin}}(A)$ and the infinite-value case excludes $\varphi(x)=\infty$. Equation <a href="#ext:eq:strict-interval" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:strict-interval">[ext:eq:strict-interval]</a> makes that inequality strict as well. Applying Equation <a href="#ext:eq:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:comparison">[ext:eq:comparison]</a> twice proves $Nu\le x\le(N+1)u$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-ext-cor-condition-c"></a>
<div id="ext:cor:condition-C" class="corollary">

**Corollary 12** (Condition (C) from comparison). *Let $A$ be a simple exact non-elementary $C^*$-algebra with strict comparison in the sense of Equation <a href="#ext:eq:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:comparison">[ext:eq:comparison]</a>. Then $\mathrm{Cu}(A)$ satisfies condition <span class="upright">(C)</span>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ext-cor-condition-c"><summary>Proof of stmt-ext-cor-condition-c</summary>

*Proof.* Theorem <a href="#ext:thm:divisibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:thm:divisibility">11</a> gives the full almost-divisibility sandwich, and Lemma <a href="#lem:comparison-convention" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:comparison-convention">6</a> gives almost unperforation. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 13*. The same sequence $(M_j)$ works for every functional. The values $\varphi([e])$ need not be uniformly bounded over any trace family: their pointwise finiteness is enough to handle $\varphi(x)=\infty$, and their bound by $\varphi(x)$ handles finite values. This is why no trace normalization is needed.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### A unital uniform-trace illustration

The simple route into (C) is complete. We record a separate consequence of trace transfer and integer rounding when traces can be normalized. It illustrates the approximation mechanism without an exactness, nuclearity, or comparison hypothesis, and is not needed in the absorption construction. The spectral discretization in <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[DadarlatToms2010](#ref-DadarlatToms2010), Section 5] provides related rank-approximation methods; here small support permits integer spectral cuts with one support-rank of error.

<a id="stmt-prop-unital-approx"></a>
<div id="prop:unital-approx" class="proposition">

**Proposition 14** (Uniform approximation from below). *Let $A$ be a unital simple infinite-dimensional $C^*$-algebra with nonempty tracial state space $T(A)$. For every $p\in A_+$ and $\eta>0$, there are a finite integer $L$ and $w\in M_L(A)_+$ such that $$0\le\tau(p)-d_\tau(w)\le\eta
 \qquad(\tau\in T(A)),$$ where matrix traces are unnormalized. No exactness, nuclearity, or comparison assumption is required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-unital-approx"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-unital-approx</summary>

*Proof.* Choose an integer $N$ with $1/N<\eta$. There are $N$ mutually orthogonal nonzero positive elements in $A$: repeatedly split a positive element at two distinct positive spectral values. If a nonzero hereditary corner had no such element, it would contain a minimal projection, and simplicity would identify $A$ with compact operators; unitality would then make $A$ finite dimensional. For any two nonzero positives $e,f$, simplicity supplies $0\ne v\in eAf$. The class $[v^*v]=[vv^*]$ is nonzero and below both $[e]$ and $[f]$. Iterating over the orthogonal family gives $0\ne h\in A_+$ with $$d_\tau(h)\le 1/N\qquad(\tau\in T(A)).$$

Fullness gives finitely many $s_i\in A$ with $\sum_i s_i^*hs_i=1$. To see the exact equality, choose an invertible finite sum $S=\sum_i r_iht_i$ approximating $1$. For the row $R=(r_ih^{1/2})_i$ and column $D=(h^{1/2}t_i)_i$, the identity $S=RD$ gives $S^*S\le\|R\|^2D^*D$, so $D^*D$ is invertible. Set $s_i=t_i(D^*D)^{-1/2}$. The element $$c=\sum_i h^{1/2}s_i p s_i^*h^{1/2}\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}_A(h)_+$$ therefore satisfies $\tau(c)=\tau(p)$ and $d_\tau(c)\le1/N$ for every $\tau\in T(A)$. Choose an integer $L>\|c\|$ and let $w=\bigoplus_{j=1}^L(c-j)_+$. Lemma <a href="#ext:lem:rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:lem:rounding">10</a>, applied in the stabilization, gives $$d_\tau(w)\le\tau(c)\le d_\tau(w)+d_\tau(c)
                       \le d_\tau(w)+1/N.$$ This proves the required simultaneous inequalities. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:support-tools"></a>
### Comparison and support tools under condition (C)

Corollary <a href="#ext:cor:condition-C" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ext:cor:condition-C">12</a> has supplied condition (C) from simple extended-functional comparison. We first complete the other route, from the canonical Cuntz-semigroup map. The remaining lemmas turn (C) into the hereditary supports, matrix cones, and bounded witnesses used by the common absorption construction. They apply without simplicity; exactness is needed for the trace comparison, and separability enters the central-sequence constructions.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The consequences of the canonical Cu map

<a id="stmt-lem-c"></a>
<div id="lem:C" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15** (Cuntz regularity). *Under the hypothesis of Corollary <a href="#cor:canonical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:canonical">3</a>, the semigroup $\mathrm{Cu}(A)$ satisfies condition <span class="upright">(C)</span>, namely Equations <a href="#eq:pre:au" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:au">[eq:pre:au]</a>–<a href="#eq:pre:div" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:div">[eq:pre:div]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-c"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-c</summary>

*Proof.* The algebra $A\otimes\mathcal Z$ and its stabilization absorb $\mathcal Z$. Rørdam’s Theorem 4.5  makes their $W$-semigroup almost unperforated. Transport through $\mathrm{Cu}(\iota)$ proves <a href="#eq:pre:au" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:au">[eq:pre:au]</a>.

For an integer $N\ge1$, Rørdam’s divisibility lemma <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Rordam2004](#ref-Rordam2004), Lemma 4.2] supplies $z\in\mathcal Z_+$ such that $$N[z]\le[1_{\mathcal Z}]\le(N+1)[z].$$

For $x=[b]$, tensor these comparisons with $b$. Comparison is preserved by tensoring with a fixed positive element <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[Rordam2004](#ref-Rordam2004), Lemma 4.1]: tensor the witnesses and use a bounded approximate unit on the other factor. Thus $$N[b\otimes z]\le[b\otimes1_{\mathcal Z}]
       \le(N+1)[b\otimes z].$$ The middle class is exactly $\mathrm{Cu}(\iota)(x)$. Pulling back the outside class proves <a href="#eq:pre:div" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:div">[eq:pre:div]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Comparison from a rank gap

The later construction will compare a defect with a matrix-cone weight by proving a uniform gap between their ranks. The following form allows traces that vanish on an ideal or take infinite values. Exactness turns the rank inequalities into functional inequalities, and almost unperforation supplies comparison through Lemma <a href="#lem:comparison-convention" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:comparison-convention">6</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-g"></a>
<div id="lem:G" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (Comparison from a uniform rank gap). *Suppose $A$ is exact and satisfies <a href="#eq:pre:au" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:au">[eq:pre:au]</a>. If $v,w\in A_+$ satisfy $$d_\tau(v)\le\tfrac12d_\tau(w)$$ for every lower semicontinuous extended trace on $A$, then $v\precsim w$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-g"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-g</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\varphi\in\mathrm F(\mathrm{Cu}(A))$. By Equation <a href="#ext:eq:trace-representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ext:eq:trace-representation">[ext:eq:trace-representation]</a>, it is represented by an extended trace on $A\otimes\mathcal K$. Restricting that trace to the original corner and applying the hypothesis gives $$\varphi([v])\le\tfrac12\varphi([w])\le\varphi([w]).$$ The inequality is strict whenever $0<\varphi([w])<\infty$. Since $\mathrm{Cu}(A)$ is almost unperforated, Lemma <a href="#lem:comparison-convention" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:comparison-convention">6</a> applies and gives $[v]\le[w]$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Placing divisible matrix cones

Almost divisibility provides equal Cuntz classes below a prescribed positive element. Cutting these classes lets us place the corresponding matrix cone inside its hereditary algebra. The two forms below retain, respectively, norm visibility in representations and a prescribed fraction of the rank for bounded traces.

<a id="stmt-lem-placement"></a>
<div id="lem:placement" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (Cone placement). *Assume <a href="#eq:pre:au" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:au">[eq:pre:au]</a>–<a href="#eq:pre:div" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:div">[eq:pre:div]</a>.*

1.  *Let $B$ be a hereditary subalgebra of a matrix algebra over $A\otimes\mathcal K$, let $b\in B_+$ be a contraction, and let $N\ge1$ and $\epsilon>0$. There is a c.p.c. order-zero map $\psi:M_N\to\mathop{\mathrm{her}}_B(b)$ such that $$\|\sigma(\psi(1))\|=1
     \quad\text{whenever}\quad
     \sigma((b-\epsilon)_+)\ne0.$$ Its $N$ diagonal weights are orthogonal and equivalent in $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}_B(b)$. Each diagonal weight, cut at $1/2$, dominates a positive class $u_0$ for which $$[(b-\epsilon)_+]\le(N+1)u_0.$$*

2.  *Let $C$ be isomorphic to a finite direct sum of hereditary subalgebras as above. For $b\in C_+$ a contraction, $k\ge1$, and $0<q\le1$, there is a c.p.c. order-zero map $\psi:M_k\to\mathop{\mathrm{her}}_C(b)$, with weight $t=\psi(1)$, such that <a id="eq:pre:P"></a>$$\label{eq:pre:P}
     d_\sigma(t)\le q\,d_\sigma(b),\qquad
     d_\sigma((t-\tfrac12)_+)
           \ge(q/3)d_\sigma((b-\tfrac12)_+)$$ for every bounded positive trace $\sigma$ on $C$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-placement"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-placement</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $u=[r]$ with $N[r]\le[b]\le(N+1)[r]$. By <a href="#eq:pre:cuts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pre:cuts">[eq:pre:cuts]</a>, a sufficiently small $\eta>0$ gives $$[(b-\epsilon)_+]\le(N+1)[(r-2\eta)_+].$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:pre:cut-placement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pre:cut-placement">5</a> places the hereditary algebra of $\operatorname{diag}((r-\eta)_+,\ldots,(r-\eta)_+)$, with $N$ diagonal entries, inside $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}_B(b)$. In this hereditary matrix algebra take the cone $e_{ij}\mapsto e_{ij}\otimes g(r)$, where $0\le g\le1$, $g=0$ on $[0,\eta]$, and $g=1$ on $[2\eta,\infty)$. Transport it by the polar isometry. Every diagonal acts as one on the corresponding transported $(r-2\eta)_+$, proving both its cut comparison and the norm-one assertion. The off-diagonal entries give equivalence of the orthogonal weights.

For the second assertion do this in each summand, with $N=\lceil k/q\rceil$ and $\epsilon=1/2$, and restrict to the first $k$ slots. The complete $N$-slot weight lies in $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(b)$, so equivalence and additivity of rank give $$d_\sigma(t)\le(k/N)d_\sigma(b)\le q\,d_\sigma(b).$$ The retained narrow classes and the cut comparison give $$d_\sigma((t-\tfrac12)_+)
 \ge\frac{k}{N+1}d_\sigma((b-\tfrac12)_+)
 \ge(q/3)d_\sigma((b-\tfrac12)_+).$$ All comparisons take place in the indicated hereditary algebra, by Lemma <a href="#lem:pre:cut-placement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pre:cut-placement">5</a>. Thus arbitrary bounded traces on that algebra may be used, whether or not they extend boundedly to $A$. Summing the finitely many summands proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### A support compression

To make an error operator small on a hereditary support, we compress its complement below a chosen threshold and take the positive part. The following observation gives the resulting bound on the support, without requiring the error operator to commute with the compressing element.

<a id="stmt-lem-support"></a>
<div id="lem:support" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18**. *Let $K=K^*$ be a bounded operator on a Hilbert space, let $s\ge0$ be bounded, and let $\lambda\in\mathbb R$. On the support of $$b=s[s(\lambda-K)s]_+s$$ the compression of $K$ is at most $\lambda$. If $b=0$, then $s(\lambda-K)s\le0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-support"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-support</summary>

*Proof.* Put $H=s(\lambda-K)s$. For a vector $\xi$ in the positive spectral space of $H$, $$\langle(\lambda-K)s\xi,s\xi\rangle
       =\langle H\xi,\xi\rangle\ge0.$$ The range closure of $sH_+^{1/2}$ is the support space of $b$. It is contained in the closure of the images $s\xi$ just used. Continuity of the quadratic form proves the compression bound.

If $b=0$, then $H_+^{1/2}s=0$. Since $H_+$ is supported on $p_s$, and the range of $s$ is dense in its support space, this implies $H_+=0$, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Ultrapowers and bounded diagonalization

Fix a free ultrafilter $\omega$ on $\mathbb N$. We write $$D_\omega=\ell^\infty(\mathbb N,D)/
   \{(x_n):\lim_{n\to\omega}\|x_n\|=0\},$$ and identify $D$ with its constant copy. For separable $A$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ann}}(A)=\{x\in A_\omega\cap A':xa=ax=0
             \text{ for every }a\in A\}.$$ An approximate unit of $A$ gives the unit in $(A_\omega\cap A')/\mathop{\mathrm{Ann}}(A)$. We use c.p.c. lifting for separable nuclear domains <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[CE1976](#ref-CE1976), Theorem 3.10 and Corollary 3.11]; in particular maps from a matrix algebra can be lifted to c.p.c. coordinate maps.

<a id="stmt-lem-diagonal"></a>
<div id="lem:diagonal" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (Bounded diagonalization). *Suppose a countable family of norm-continuous algebraic and functional-calculus conditions, with fixed coefficients in a norm ultrapower, is approximately satisfiable on every finite subfamily. Suppose each unknown has a fixed individual norm bound in these finite solutions. Then the conditions can be satisfied exactly in the ultrapower. Additional countably many commutator conditions and conditions after multiplication by a fixed countable subset of the algebra may be imposed in the same way.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-diagonal"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-diagonal</summary>

*Proof.* For each $j$, choose an ultrapower solution of the first $j$ conditions to accuracy $1/j$, and choose bounded coordinate representatives. Slightly enlarging each fixed bound once, and truncating representatives, gives bounds independent of $j$. There is an $\omega$-large set on which these first $j$ coordinate conditions hold to accuracy $2/j$. Replace these sets by decreasing finite intersections, also intersecting the $j$-th set with $\{n\ge j\}$. They have empty intersection. At coordinate $n$, use the solution of the largest applicable level $j\le n$, with an arbitrary bounded choice when no level applies. The chosen level tends to infinity along $\omega$; each fixed condition therefore has error tending to zero. The bounds ensure that every unknown represents an element of the norm ultrapower. Positivity or complete positivity can be preserved by choosing positive or completely positive lifts at each level. ◻

</details>

We will also use the following reindexing observation. If a central sequence is to commute with finitely many already chosen coordinate values, then, at each output coordinate, those values are ordinary elements of $A$. Centrality supplies the required good set of old indices. It can be intersected with finitely many other good sets before choosing the new index. Any uniform coordinate predicate retained in this selection continues to hold. In the nonunital quotient, algebraic and unit defects are tested after multiplication by a dense subset of $A$; boundedness then extends the resulting relations to all of $A$.

<a id="shr:section"></a>
### Shrinking two hereditary supports

We will shrink two hereditary supports so that a prescribed cross-compression decreases by a fixed factor, while at least one new support remains nonzero in each irreducible representation where an original element has norm at least $1/2$. Repeated use of this operation will separate the supports in the norm-cone construction of Section <a href="#norm:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:section">8</a>. Only the two Cuntz-semigroup properties in <span class="upright">(C)</span> are needed; nuclearity is not used here. All Cuntz comparisons in hereditary subalgebras are understood in the ambient stabilization, with their witnesses compressed into the indicated corners as in the preliminaries. If $\sigma$ is a representation, its matrix amplifications will also be denoted by $\sigma$.

<a id="stmt-lem-shrinking"></a>
<div id="lem:shrinking" class="lemma">

<a id="shr:conclusion"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Two-support shrinking). *There is a numerical constant $0<c<1$ with the following property for every $C^*$-algebra $A$ satisfying <span class="upright">(C)</span>. If $a,b\in A$ are positive contractions and $z\in\widetilde A$ satisfies $\|p_a z p_b\|\le1$, there are positive contractions $a'\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(a)$ and $b'\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(b)$ such that $$\label{shr:conclusion}
 \|p_{a'}z p_{b'}\|\le c$$ and, for every irreducible representation $\sigma$ of $A$, <a id="shr:visibility"></a>$$\label{shr:visibility}
 \max\{\|\sigma(a)\|,\|\sigma(b)\|\}\ge\tfrac12
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \sigma(a')\ne0\ \text{or}\ \sigma(b')\ne0.$$ The support norms are computed in the bidual, or equivalently in the universal representation.*

</div>

We first prove a transfer statement for two hereditary supports. It replaces finitely many rectangular elements by one contraction which has norm one in every representation where a prescribed element reaches a fixed norm threshold.

<a id="stmt-lem-transfer"></a>
<div id="lem:transfer" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21** (Transfer between two supports). *Suppose that $A$ satisfies <span class="upright">(C)</span>. Let $B\subseteq A$ be hereditary, let $D\in M_l(B)_+$ and $E\in B_+$, and suppose that $w_1,\ldots,w_m\in M_{l,1}(B)$ satisfy $$w_iw_i^*\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(D),\qquad w_i^*w_i\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(E).$$ For each $\delta>0$ there is a contraction $v\in M_{l,1}(B)$ such that $$vv^*\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(D),\qquad v^*v\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(E),$$ and, in every representation $\sigma$ of $B$, $$\max_{1\le i\le m}\|\sigma(w_i)\|\ge\delta
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \|\sigma(v)\|=1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-transfer</summary>

<a id="shr:transfer-cut"></a>
*Proof.* Set $T_0=\operatorname{diag}(w_1^*w_1,\ldots,w_m^*w_m)$. The hereditary conditions and $[w_i^*w_i]=[w_iw_i^*]$ give $$[T_0]\le m[E],\qquad [T_0]\le m[D].$$ By divisibility in <span class="upright">(C)</span>, choose a positive element $r$ in a stabilization with $$(m+1)[r]\le[T_0]\le(m+2)[r].$$ Almost unperforation, applied with $m$, gives $[r]\le[E]$ and $[r]\le[D]$. Choose $\eta>0$ sufficiently small that $$\label{shr:transfer-cut}
 [(T_0-\delta^2/2)_+]\le(m+2)[(r-2\eta)_+].$$ This is possible because the class on the left is compactly contained in $[T_0]$, and the classes $(m+2)[(r-2\eta)_+]$ increase to $(m+2)[r]$.

The cut-placement result in the preliminaries supplies partial isometries $u_E,u_D$ which identify $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}((r-\eta)_+)$ with hereditary subalgebras of $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(E)$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(D)$, respectively. Their initial projection is the support of $(r-\eta)_+$. Choose a continuous function $j$ with $0\le j\le1$, $j=0$ on $[0,\eta]$, and $j=1$ on $[2\eta,\infty)$. Then $$v=u_D j(r)u_E^*$$ belongs to $M_{l,1}(B)$: factor it as $(u_Dj(r)^{1/2})(u_Ej(r)^{1/2})^*$. Each factor belongs to the ambient stabilized algebra by cut placement, and the product’s left and right supports lie in the prescribed finite matrix corners. It is a contraction, and $$v^*v=u_Ej(r)^2u_E^*\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(E),\qquad
 vv^*=u_Dj(r)^2u_D^*\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(D).$$ If $\|\sigma(w_i)\|\ge\delta$ for some $i$, then $\sigma((T_0-\delta^2/2)_+)\ne0$. By <a href="#shr:transfer-cut" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:transfer-cut">[shr:transfer-cut]</a>, the source copy $u_E(r-2\eta)_+u_E^*$ is nonzero in this representation: otherwise the comparison would make the represented left side zero. The operator $\sigma(v^*v)$ is the identity on the support of that nonzero copy, since $j$ is one on its spectrum. Thus $\|\sigma(v)\|=1$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-transfer-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-transfer</summary>

*Proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:shrinking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:shrinking">20</a>.* Suppose that no such constant exists. Fix numbers $c_n\in(0,1)$ increasing to $1$. For each $n$, choose an algebra satisfying <span class="upright">(C)</span> and data $a,b,z$ for which the conclusion fails with $c=c_n$. The algebras are allowed to vary; we suppress $n$ until the limiting argument. The construction below always gives <a href="#shr:conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:conclusion">[shr:conclusion]</a>, so failure will provide a representation in which both constructed positives vanish.

<a id="shr:d-visibility"></a>
<a id="shr:Q-visibility"></a>
**Step 1: a matrix corner and two prospective supports.** Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:placement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:placement">17</a>, with seven slots and a cut of $a$ below $1/2$, to obtain a c.p.c. order-zero map $M_7\to\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(a)$ with weight $d$ satisfying $$\label{shr:d-visibility}
 \|\sigma(a)\|\ge\tfrac12\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\|\sigma(d)\|=1.$$ Its supporting matrix units identify $$\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(d)=M_7(B),\qquad d=\operatorname{diag}(P),$$ where $B$ is the hereditary algebra generated by its first diagonal weight and $P\in B$ is a strictly positive contraction. Concretely, in any representation, the support space of $d$ is identified with $\mathcal H^{\oplus7}$, where $B$ acts nondegenerately on $\mathcal H$. Choose $Q=\min(1,2b)$ by functional calculus. Then $Q\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(b)$ is a positive contraction and $$\label{shr:Q-visibility}
 \|\sigma(b)\|\ge\tfrac12\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\|\sigma(Q)\|=1.$$

<a id="shr:U"></a>
<a id="shr:ab-prime"></a>
We shall choose contractions $v_0\in M_{2,1}(B)$ and $v_1\in M_{3,1}(B)$. For any such choice set, in multiplier notation, $$\label{shr:U}
 U=\begin{pmatrix}
 \frac12\,1\\[2pt] \frac12 v_0\\[2pt] \frac12 v_1\\[2pt]
 \bigl(\frac34\,1-\frac14v_0^*v_0-\frac14v_1^*v_1\bigr)^{1/2}
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad
 L=UPU^*\in M_7(B),\qquad t=\tfrac12.$$ The expression under the square root is at least $\frac14\,1$, and $U^*U=1$. Define $$\label{shr:ab-prime}
 a'=(L-t)_+,\qquad
 b'=Q\bigl[Q(c_n^2t-z^*Lz)Q\bigr]_+Q.$$ These belong to $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(a)$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(b)$, respectively. They are contractions: $0\le L\le1$, and the selfadjoint operator inside the positive part in the second expression is bounded above by $c_n^2t\,1$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:support">18</a>, the compression of $z^*Lz$ to the support of $b'$ is at most $c_n^2t$. Since $L\ge t p_{a'}$, this implies $$t p_{b'}z^*p_{a'}z p_{b'}
 \le p_{b'}z^*Lz p_{b'}\le c_n^2t p_{b'},$$ which proves <a href="#shr:conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:conclusion">[shr:conclusion]</a> with $c_n$, for every choice of the two columns.

<a id="shr:Z-bound"></a>
**Step 2: data chosen before either column.** Put $Z=p_d zQ$ and write its rows in groups of sizes $1,2,3,1$ as $Z_{\mathrm f},Z_0,Z_1,Z_{\mathrm{last}}$. These are operators from the original representation space to the corresponding copies of $\mathcal H$. The hypothesis on $z$ gives $$\label{shr:Z-bound}
 Z^*Z\le Q^2,$$ because $p_d\le p_a$ and $p_bQ=Q$. Thus $Z$ has norm at most one, even though no uniform bound on $z$ itself is assumed.

<a id="shr:alignment-chain"></a>
The next choices address the only way the prospective supports can fail to retain visibility. For any choice of the columns, a representation in which $a'=b'=0$ would have $P\le t$ and, by the zero case of Lemma <a href="#lem:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:support">18</a>, $$\label{shr:alignment-chain}
 c_n^2tQ^2\le Z^*UPU^*Z\le tZ^*Z\le tQ^2.$$ As $c_n\to1$, these inequalities force the limiting range of $Z$ into the range of $U$, with its $U$-coordinate in the $t$-spectral subspace of $P$. Step 5 proves this alignment precisely. Since the first row of $U$ is $1/2$, the first row of $Z$ then recovers that coordinate. The following cutoffs implement this recovery within the hereditary corner, before either column has been chosen.

<a id="shr:initial-bounds"></a>
Fix continuous functions on $[0,1]$ by $$g(x)=\min\{1,\max\{0,8x-2\}\},\qquad G(x)=\min\{1,4x\}.$$ They vanish at zero, satisfy $Gg=g$, and $g(t)=G(t)=1$. Set <a id="shr:XY"></a>$$\label{shr:XY}
 X=2g(P)Z_{\mathrm f},\qquad h_0=XX^*,\qquad
 Y_i=2\operatorname{diag}(G(P))Z_iX^*\quad(i=0,1).$$ Here $h_0\in B_+$, $Y_0\in M_{2,1}(B)$, and $Y_1\in M_{3,1}(B)$. For example, their entries are the appropriate entries of sandwiches of $zQ^2z^*$ by $g(d)$ and $G(d)$; these sandwiches belong to $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(d)$. We have $$\label{shr:initial-bounds}
 \|X\|\le2,\quad \|h_0\|\le4,\quad \|Y_i\|\le4,
 \qquad G(P)h_0=h_0=h_0G(P).$$ In particular, $G(P)$ acts as the identity on the support of $h_0$. None of these data depends on $v_0$ or $v_1$.

<a id="shr:BT-y"></a>
For later use fix $$r=\tfrac18,\qquad
 W_j=r^{-1}(Y_0)_j^*\quad(j=1,2),\qquad
 f=f(h_0),\quad f(x)=\min\{1,\max\{0,4x-1\}\}\quad(x\ge0).$$ Thus $f$ vanishes on $[0,1/4]$ and is one on $[1/2,\infty)$. With $u=W_1f$ and $c_f=(1-f^2)^{1/2}$, put $V=u+c_f$ in $\widetilde B$ and define <a id="shr:T"></a>$$\label{shr:T}
 T=1-VV^*=f^2-uu^*-u c_f-c_f u^*\in B.$$ This selfadjoint element records the prospective range complement of $V$. At this stage $V$ need not be an isometry and $T$ need not be positive. Step 7 will produce a reducing subspace in the failure limit on which $V$ is an isometry and $T$ is a nonzero projection. We use the positive spectral part of $T$ now to prepare the second column, which will rule out that possibility. The bounds above give $\|W_j\|\le32$, $\|u\|\le32$, and $\|T\|\le1089$. Fix on $\mathbb R$ the continuous functions $$\beta(x)=\min\{1,\max\{0,2x-1\}\},\qquad
 \chi(x)=\min\{1,\max\{0,4x-1\}\}.$$ They vanish at zero, $\beta$ vanishes on $(-\infty,1/2]$, $\beta(1)=1$, and $\chi\beta=\beta$. Put $$\label{shr:BT-y}
 b_T=\beta(T),\qquad B_T=\mathop{\mathrm{her}}_B(b_T),\qquad
 y=\operatorname{diag}(b_T)Y_1b_T\in M_{3,1}(B_T),\qquad
 S=\operatorname{diag}(b_T)\in M_3(B_T).$$ We have $\|y\|\le4$, and $S$ is strictly positive in $M_3(B_T)$.

<a id="shr:k-comparison"></a>
<a id="shr:bounded-witnesses"></a>
**Step 3: the second column and bounded comparison witnesses.** Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:placement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:placement">17</a> with two slots inside $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}((yy^*-10^{-3})_+)$, using the narrower cut $(yy^*-10^{-2})_+$. The plateau of the slot weights may be chosen equal to one on the transported narrower element. To use the contraction form of that Lemma explicitly, take $b=(yy^*-10^{-3})_+/16$ and $\epsilon=9\cdot10^{-3}/16$; positive rescaling preserves all the hereditary algebras and Cuntz classes in question. We obtain equivalent orthogonal positive contractions $k,k'\in M_3(B_T)$ with $$\label{shr:k-comparison}
 k,k'\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}((yy^*-10^{-3})_+),\qquad
 [(yy^*-10^{-2})_+]\le3[(k-0.9)_+].$$ If this hereditary algebra is zero, take $k=k'=0$; the same assertions hold. Choose three matrices $x_j\in M_3(B_T)$ witnessing $$\left\|(yy^*-0.01)_+-\sum_{j=1}^3x_j^*(k-0.9)_+x_j\right\|<0.005.$$ There are three matrices because the right side of the comparison is a threefold direct sum; rectangular witnesses can be split into its three blocks. Replace $x_j$ by $$q_j=\left(\frac{(k-0.9)_+}{(k-0.8)_+}\right)^{1/2}x_j,$$ where the quotient is defined to be zero when its denominator is zero. This is a continuous function of $k$. Then $$\label{shr:bounded-witnesses}
 \left\|(yy^*-0.01)_+-\sum_{j=1}^3q_j^*(k-0.8)_+q_j\right\|<0.005,
 \qquad \|q_j\|\le\sqrt{160}<13.$$ Indeed the compression sum before replacement is positive and has norm less than $16$, so each summand has norm less than $16$. Also $$\frac{(k-0.9)_+}{(k-0.8)_+}\le10(k-0.9)_+.$$ These give $q_j^*q_j\le10x_j^*(k-0.9)_+x_j$ and the stated bound. The positive gap between the two cuts is what makes these witnesses uniformly bounded.

<a id="shr:Dprime-comparison"></a>
Choose $p'=\min(1,k/0.8)$ and put $$D'=(1-p')S(1-p')\in M_3(B_T)_+.$$ We claim that $$\label{shr:Dprime-comparison}
 3[b_T]=[S]\le[k]+[D']\le2[D'].$$ For the first inequality, sandwich $\frac12\,1\le(p')^2+(1-p')^2$ by $S^{1/2}$. The resulting two summands have the classes of $p'Sp'$ and $(1-p')S(1-p')$, and $p'Sp'\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(k)$. For the second inequality it suffices to check that $k'\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(D')$, since $[k]=[k']$. Orthogonality gives $(1-p')k'(1-p')=k'$. Since $S$ is strictly positive, there are positive elements $c_j\to k'$ with $c_j\le C_jS$ for finite constants $C_j$: one may take $c_j=e_jk'e_j$ with $e_j=S(S+1/j)^{-1}$. Consequently $$(1-p')c_j(1-p')\le C_jD',\qquad
 (1-p')c_j(1-p')\longrightarrow k',$$ which proves the inclusion and <a href="#shr:Dprime-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:Dprime-comparison">[shr:Dprime-comparison]</a>.

<a id="shr:v1"></a>
<a id="shr:v1-annihilation"></a>
<a id="shr:fixed-supports"></a>
Almost unperforation now gives $[b_T]\le[D']$. Cut placement supplies a column $v_1\in M_{3,1}(B_T)$ with $$\label{shr:v1}
 v_1^*v_1=(b_T-1/4)_+,\qquad
 v_1v_1^*\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(D'),\qquad \|v_1\|\le1.$$ Since $(k-0.8)_+(1-p')=0$, its range condition implies $$\label{shr:v1-annihilation}
 (k-0.8)_+v_1=0.$$ We shall also retain the following fixed continuous support identities: $$\label{shr:fixed-supports}
 (10^{-3}-yy^*)_+k=0,\qquad
 \chi(T)(v_1)_j=(v_1)_j=(v_1)_j\chi(T)\quad(1\le j\le3).$$ The first follows from <a href="#shr:k-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:k-comparison">[shr:k-comparison]</a>; the second holds because $\chi(T)b_T=b_T$ and the entries of $v_1$ belong to $B_T$. These equations, unlike statements about varying support projections, pass directly to an operator ultraproduct.

**Step 4: finite word tests and the first column.** Let $p$ be the continuous function of $Y_0Y_0^*$ which is zero up to $r^2$, linear between $r^2$ and $(r+1/n)^2$, and one thereafter. Put <a id="shr:DE"></a>$$\label{shr:DE}
 s=\operatorname{diag}((h_0-1/4)_+)\in M_2(B),\qquad
 E=(h_0-1/2)_+\in B,\qquad
 D=s\bigl[s(\tfrac14-p)s\bigr]_+s\in M_2(B).$$ Choose an ordered alphabet consisting of all entries of <a id="shr:alphabet"></a>$$\label{shr:alphabet}
 P,h_0,Y_0,Y_1,p,s,E,D,T,b_T,y,k,k',D',v_1,q_1,q_2,q_3$$ and their adjoints. The number of letters is independent of $n$, as all matrix sizes are fixed. Their norms are bounded independently of the instance: the bounds already given apply, $\|s\|,\|E\|\le4$, $\|D\|\le256$, and $p,k,k',b_T,D',v_1$ are contractions. Thus, for example, $1089$ is a common bound on every letter. Neither $v_0$ nor $U$ is a letter.

<a id="shr:word-tests"></a>
<a id="shr:v0-fixed"></a>
Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transfer">21</a> to the finite list of columns $$\label{shr:word-tests}
 D\mathbf e_jxE,\qquad j=1,2,\quad
 x\text{ a word of length at most }n,$$ including the empty word $x=1$, with threshold $1/n$. Here $\mathbf e_j$ is the $j$th scalar basis column. The left and right squared moduli of these columns lie in $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(D)$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(E)$, respectively. The resulting contraction $v_0\in M_{2,1}(B)$ satisfies <a id="shr:v0"></a>$$\label{shr:v0}
 v_0v_0^*\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(D),\qquad v_0^*v_0\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(E),\qquad
 v_0^*pv_0\le\tfrac14v_0^*v_0.$$ The last assertion is Lemma <a href="#lem:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:support">18</a> applied to the pencil defining $D$. In addition, we have the exact operator identities $$\label{shr:v0-fixed}
 (1/2-h_0)_+v_0^*=0,\qquad
 \operatorname{diag}(G(P))v_0=v_0.$$ For the first use the right hereditary condition. For the second, the support of $D$ is contained in that of $s$, and $G(P)$ fixes the support of $h_0$ by <a href="#shr:initial-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:initial-bounds">[shr:initial-bounds]</a>. The choice of $v_1$, all the comparison witnesses, and the whole alphabet precedes the choice of $v_0$, so the construction is not circular.

**Step 5: failure forces alignment in a Hilbert ultraproduct.** Make the choices above at coordinate $n$ and define $a',b'$ by <a href="#shr:ab-prime" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:ab-prime">[shr:ab-prime]</a>. Since they satisfy the required angle bound, the assumed failure gives an irreducible representation $\sigma_n$ such that $$\max\{\|\sigma_n(a)\|,\|\sigma_n(b)\|\}\ge\tfrac12,
 \qquad \sigma_n(a')=\sigma_n(b')=0.$$ Use the same symbols for the represented operators. The first vanishing gives $L\le t$, hence $P=U^*LU\le t$. By <a href="#shr:d-visibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:d-visibility">[shr:d-visibility]</a>, the $a$-trigger is impossible. Therefore $\|Q\|=1$ by <a href="#shr:Q-visibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:Q-visibility">[shr:Q-visibility]</a>. The two vanishings also give the conditional inequalities <a href="#shr:alignment-chain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:alignment-chain">[shr:alignment-chain]</a> established in Step 2.

Pass along the fixed free ultrafilter to Hilbert-space ultraproducts of the representation spaces and of their first matrix-coordinate spaces. For clarity, such an ultraproduct is the completion of bounded sequences of vectors modulo those whose norms tend to zero along the ultrafilter, with inner product the ultralimit of the coordinate inner products. Uniformly bounded operator sequences act on these spaces, respect sums, products and adjoints, and have norm equal to the ultralimit of their norms. The last assertion follows by choosing a nearly norm-maximizing unit vector at each coordinate. Fixed continuous functional calculus is preserved, by uniform polynomial approximation on a common spectral interval. All operators used here are uniformly bounded: in addition to the alphabet this includes $Q,Z,X,U,v_0$, by <a href="#shr:Z-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:Z-bound">[shr:Z-bound]</a>, <a href="#shr:initial-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:initial-bounds">[shr:initial-bounds]</a>, and <a href="#shr:U" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:U">[shr:U]</a>. We keep the same notation for their limits.

<a id="shr:limit-Y"></a>
Equality holds throughout <a href="#shr:alignment-chain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:alignment-chain">[shr:alignment-chain]</a>. In particular $Z^*Z=Q^2$ and $Z^*(t1-UPU^*)Z=0$. Since $$t1-UPU^*=t(1-UU^*)+U(t-P)U^*$$ is a sum of positive operators, both positive summands annihilate $Z$. For $\xi=U^*Z$, it follows that <a id="shr:alignment"></a>$$\label{shr:alignment}
 Z=U\xi,\qquad P\xi=t\xi,\qquad \xi^*\xi=Q^2.$$ The first row of $U$ is $1/2$, so $X=2g(P)Z_{\mathrm f}=\xi$. Consequently $$\label{shr:limit-Y}
 \|h_0\|=1,\qquad
 Y_i=\operatorname{diag}(G(P))v_i h_0,\qquad
 Y_0=v_0h_0.$$ The last equality uses the second identity in <a href="#shr:v0-fixed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:v0-fixed">[shr:v0-fixed]</a>.

<a id="shr:moving-cutoff"></a>
<a id="shr:p-small"></a>
<a id="shr:R0"></a>
**Step 6: the moving cutoff makes every word test vanish.** Although the function defining $p$ changes with $n$, $p$ itself is a bounded alphabet variable. At each coordinate it commutes with $R_0=Y_0Y_0^*$ and satisfies $$(r^2-R_0)_+p=0,\qquad
 \|(1-p)(R_0-r^2)_+\|\le(r+1/n)^2-r^2.$$ Thus in the limit $$\label{shr:moving-cutoff}
 [p,R_0]=0,\qquad (r^2-R_0)_+p=0,\qquad
 (1-p)(R_0-r^2)_+=0.$$ The first two equations show that the support of $p$ lies in the spectral subspace of $R_0$ for $[r^2,\infty)$, hence in the closure of the range of $Y_0$, and therefore of $v_0$ by <a href="#shr:limit-Y" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:limit-Y">[shr:limit-Y]</a>. On this last closed range the inequality $v_0^*pv_0\le\frac14v_0^*v_0$ says that the quadratic form of $p$ is at most one quarter of the squared norm. This follows first on vectors $v_0\eta$, then by density; on the orthogonal complement $p$ is zero. Hence $$\label{shr:p-small}
 0\le p\le\tfrac14\,1.$$ The last equation in <a href="#shr:moving-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:moving-cutoff">[shr:moving-cutoff]</a>, and invertibility of $1-p$, now give $R_0\le r^2$. The other support equation then gives $$\label{shr:R0}
 \|Y_0\|\le r,\qquad R_0p=r^2p.$$

<a id="shr:all-word-tests"></a>
Every fixed word $x$ in the limiting alphabet satisfies $$\label{shr:all-word-tests}
 D\mathbf e_jxE=0\qquad(j=1,2).$$ Indeed, if such an operator had norm $\delta>0$, its coordinate norms would be at least $\delta/2$ on an ultrafilter-large set. On an ultrafilter-large subset we also have $n\ge\operatorname{length}(x)$ and $1/n\le\delta/2$. The transfer condition used in <a href="#shr:word-tests" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:word-tests">[shr:word-tests]</a> would give $\|v_0\|=1$ in the ultraproduct. The first equation in <a href="#shr:v0-fixed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:v0-fixed">[shr:v0-fixed]</a> puts the range of $v_0^*$ in the $h_0\ge1/2$ spectral subspace. Inverting $h_0$ on that subspace, with inverse norm at most two, gives $$\|v_0h_0\|\ge\tfrac12\|v_0\|=\tfrac12.$$ This contradicts $Y_0=v_0h_0$ and <a href="#shr:R0" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:R0">[shr:R0]</a>, since $r=1/8$.

<a id="shr:W-range"></a>
**Step 7: a nonzero defect projection on the word space.** Let $\mathcal H$ denote the limiting single-coordinate Hilbert space and set $$\mathcal L=\overline{\operatorname{span}}
 \{xE\eta:x\text{ a word in the alphabet},\ \eta\in\mathcal H\}.$$ The alphabet includes adjoints, so $\mathcal L$ reduces every letter and the $C^*$-algebra they generate. Restrict letters to this space, and matrix variables to its amplifications. Equation <a href="#shr:all-word-tests" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:all-word-tests">[shr:all-word-tests]</a> gives $D=0$ on $\mathcal L^{\oplus2}$. Moreover $E\mathcal H\subseteq\mathcal L$, so the restriction retains $\|E\|=1/2$, hence $\|h_0\|=1$. We do not assert that $v_0$ reduces $\mathcal L$. All further calculations involve only alphabet operators and their continuous functions. The needed range information from $v_0$ has already become the identity $$\label{shr:W-range}
 (1/2-h_0)_+W_j=0\qquad(j=1,2),$$ which follows on the full limiting space from $W_j=r^{-1}h_0(v_0)_j^*$ and <a href="#shr:v0-fixed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:v0-fixed">[shr:v0-fixed]</a>, and thus restricts to $\mathcal L$.

<a id="shr:Rs"></a>
By the zero case of Lemma <a href="#lem:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:support">18</a>, $D=0$ implies $s(\frac14-p)s\le0$. Equation <a href="#shr:p-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:p-small">[shr:p-small]</a> gives the reverse inequality, so $s(\frac14-p)s=0$. Taking a positive square root shows $(\frac14-p)s=0$, or $ps=s/4$. Together with <a href="#shr:R0" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:R0">[shr:R0]</a>, this yields $$\label{shr:Rs}
 R_0s=r^2s.$$ Let $e=1_{(1/4,\infty)}(h_0)$ on $\mathcal L$. The range of $s$ is dense in $(e\mathcal L)^{\oplus2}$, so <a href="#shr:Rs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:Rs">[shr:Rs]</a> implies $$(W_i e)^*(W_j e)=\delta_{ij}e\qquad(1\le i,j\le2).$$ Thus $W_1,W_2$ restricted to $e\mathcal L$ are isometries with orthogonal ranges. By <a href="#shr:W-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:W-range">[shr:W-range]</a>, their ranges lie in the $h_0\ge1/2$ subspace, where $f=1$.

<a id="shr:T-projection"></a>
It follows that $u=W_1f$ satisfies $u^*u=f^2$ and $c_fu=0$, hence also $u^*c_f=0$. Consequently $$V^*V=f^2+c_f^2=1.$$ Its range-complement projection is exactly $$\label{shr:T-projection}
 1-VV^*=f^2-uu^*-uc_f-c_fu^*=T.$$ Both the initial and final supports of $u$ are contained in $e$, and $f=ef$. As $c_f$ commutes with $e$, formula <a href="#shr:T-projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:T-projection">[shr:T-projection]</a> shows $T=eTe$. In particular <a id="shr:ThT"></a>$$\label{shr:ThT}
 Th_0T\ge\tfrac14 T.$$ This projection is nonzero. Indeed $W_2f\ne0$ because $\|h_0\|=\|f\|=1$ and $W_2$ is isometric on $e\mathcal L$, whereas $$V^*W_2f=fW_1^*W_2f+c_fW_2f=0.$$ Its nonzero range is therefore contained in the range of $T$.

<a id="shr:final-y"></a>
**Step 8: the second column gives the contradiction.** Since $T$ is now a projection, $b_T=\beta(T)=T$ and $\chi(T)=T$. The fixed identities <a href="#shr:fixed-supports" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:fixed-supports">[shr:fixed-supports]</a> give $$T(v_1)_j=(v_1)_j=(v_1)_jT.$$ They hold after restriction because $v_1$ was included in the alphabet. As $G(P)$ fixes the support of $h_0$ and $T=eTe$, it also fixes $T$; thus $\operatorname{diag}(G(P))v_1=v_1$. Using <a href="#shr:BT-y" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:BT-y">[shr:BT-y]</a>, <a href="#shr:v1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:v1">[shr:v1]</a>, and <a href="#shr:limit-Y" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:limit-Y">[shr:limit-Y]</a>, we obtain $$\label{shr:final-y}
 y=v_1Th_0T,\qquad v_1^*v_1=\tfrac34 T,\qquad
 y^*y=\tfrac34(Th_0T)^2\ge\tfrac{3}{64}T.$$ In particular, $\|yy^*\|\ge3/64$.

The three uniformly bounded witnesses in <a href="#shr:bounded-witnesses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:bounded-witnesses">[shr:bounded-witnesses]</a> survive both the ultraproduct and the restriction, giving $$\left\|(yy^*-0.01)_+-\sum_{j=1}^3q_j^*(k-0.8)_+q_j\right\|\le0.005.$$ Hence $q=(k-0.8)_+$ is nonzero: otherwise $\|yy^*\|\le0.015<3/64$, contradicting <a href="#shr:final-y" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:final-y">[shr:final-y]</a>. On the other hand, <a href="#shr:v1-annihilation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:v1-annihilation">[shr:v1-annihilation]</a> and <a href="#shr:final-y" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:final-y">[shr:final-y]</a> imply $qy=0$. The first fixed support identity in <a href="#shr:fixed-supports" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="shr:fixed-supports">[shr:fixed-supports]</a> puts the range of $k$, and hence of $q$, in the spectral subspace of $yy^*$ for $[10^{-3},\infty)$. Therefore $$0=qyy^*q\ge10^{-3}q^2,$$ forcing $q=0$. This contradiction proves the existence of the constant $c$ and completes the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="norm:section"></a>
### Norm-visible central matrix cones

The construction in this section detects a prescribed positive element uniformly in the irreducible representations at each coordinate. Its building blocks are finite word spaces at pure states. Lemma <a href="#lem:shrinking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:shrinking">20</a> lets us separate their hereditary supports while retaining all the representations that must be detected.

<a id="stmt-prop-norm"></a>
<div id="prop:norm" class="proposition">

**Proposition 22**. *Suppose that $A$ is separable and satisfies <span class="upright">(C)</span>. For every positive contraction $x\in A$, every $\gamma>0$, and every integer $k\ge2$, there are c.p.c. maps $\nu_n:M_k\to A$ such that $$\nu:M_k\longrightarrow A_\omega\cap A',
 \qquad \nu(z)=[(\nu_n(z))_n],$$ is order zero, and there is a number $\kappa>0$ for which <a id="norm:coordinate-visibility"></a>$$\label{norm:coordinate-visibility}
 \begin{gathered}
 \text{for every }n\in\mathbb N\text{ and every irreducible representation }\sigma
 \text{ of }A,\\
 \|\sigma(x)\|\ge\gamma
 \ \Longrightarrow\
 \|\sigma(x\nu_n(1))\|\ge\kappa .
 \end{gathered}$$ One may take $\kappa=\gamma^2/32$. In particular, the estimate holds on one $\omega$-large set of coordinates simultaneously for every irreducible representation, including representations chosen separately at each coordinate.*

</div>

We first establish the uniformity and compression facts used in the proof. All support projections below are taken in the bidual, or in the universal representation, and each representation is extended normally to the bidual when support estimates are used.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Finite covers and pure-state compression

For $x\in A_+$ and $\gamma>0$, write $$\mathcal K(x,\gamma)
   =\{\sigma:\sigma\text{ is an irreducible representation of }A,\
                    \|\sigma(x)\|\ge\gamma\}.$$ There is no compactness assumption on the primitive ideal space.

<a id="stmt-norm-finite-cover"></a>
<div id="norm:finite-cover" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23**. *Let $x\in A_+$, $\gamma>0$, and let $(s_\alpha)_{\alpha\in\Lambda}$ be positive elements of $A$. Suppose that $\mathcal K(x,\gamma)\ne\varnothing$ and that, for every $\sigma\in\mathcal K(x,\gamma)$, some $\sigma(s_\alpha)$ is nonzero. Then there are $\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_m$ and $t>0$ such that $$\sigma\in\mathcal K(x,\gamma)
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \max_{1\le i\le m}\|\sigma(s_{\alpha_i})\|>t.$$ Thus the nonvanishing loci of the cuts $(s_{\alpha_i}-t)_+$ still cover $\mathcal K(x,\gamma)$. For a finite covering family of positive contractions, continuous functional calculus can replace its members by positive contractions with the same supports and with maximum represented norm equal to one at every $\sigma\in\mathcal K(x,\gamma)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-norm-finite-cover"><summary>Proof of stmt-norm-finite-cover</summary>

*Proof.* Let $I$ be the closed ideal generated by all the $s_\alpha$, and let $q:A\to A/I$ be the quotient map. We claim that $\|q(x)\|<\gamma$. Otherwise, a pure state of the unitization of $A/I$ can be chosen to take the value $\|q(x)\|$ on $q(x)$. For completeness, the states taking that value form a nonempty compact face of the state space: nonemptiness follows by extending the norm-attaining state of the commutative algebra generated by $q(x)$ and the unit. An extreme point of that face is a pure state. Its value on $q(x)$ is positive, so its irreducible representation restricts nontrivially to $A/I$. Pulling that representation back to $A$ gives an element of $\mathcal K(x,\gamma)$ annihilating every $s_\alpha$, a contradiction.

The strict quotient-norm inequality permits a finite sum $$y=\sum_{j=1}^r u_j s_{\alpha(j)}v_j,\qquad
 u_j,v_j\in\widetilde A,\qquad \|x-y\|<\gamma .$$ Indeed, these finite sums are dense in $I$, and the distance from $x$ to $I$ is strictly less than $\gamma$. Choose $t>0$ so small that $$y_t=\sum_{j=1}^r u_j(s_{\alpha(j)}-t)_+v_j
 \quad\text{satisfies}\quad \|x-y_t\|<\gamma .$$ If a representation annihilates every cut appearing in this sum, then $\|\sigma(x)\|\le\|x-y_t\|<\gamma$. This proves the first assertions.

For the last assertion apply what was just proved to the given finite family and put $f_t(u)=\min\{u/t,1\}$ for $u\ge0$. The function $f_t$ vanishes only at zero, so it preserves supports. Whenever $\|\sigma(s_i)\|>t$, functional calculus gives $\|\sigma(f_t(s_i))\|=1$. ◻

</details>

The following compression is the pure-state excision principle of Akemann, Anderson, and Pedersen <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[AAP1986](#ref-AAP1986), Proposition 2.2]. We include a proof using Kadison’s transitivity theorem and the pure-state kernel identity <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[Kadison1957](#ref-Kadison1957), Theorem 1 and Corollary 2].

<a id="stmt-norm-excision"></a>
<div id="norm:excision" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Pure-state compression). *Let $\lambda$ be a pure state of a unital $C^*$-algebra $D$, with irreducible GNS representation $(\pi,\mathcal H,\xi)$. Given a finite subset $\mathcal E\subset D$ and $\epsilon>0$, there is a positive contraction $q\in D$ such that $$\pi(q)\xi=\xi,\qquad
 \|qyq-\lambda(y)q^2\|<\epsilon\quad(y\in\mathcal E).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-norm-excision"><summary>Proof of stmt-norm-excision</summary>

*Proof.* Set $$J=\{z\in D:\pi(z)\xi=0\},\qquad H=J\cap J^*.$$ The space $J$ is a closed left ideal, and $H$ is a hereditary $C^*$-subalgebra. For the latter assertion, it is a closed selfadjoint subalgebra, and if $0\le a\le b$ with $b\in H_+$, then $\lambda(a)\le\lambda(b)=0$, so $\pi(a)\xi=0$.

<a id="norm:state-kernel"></a>
We show that $$\label{norm:state-kernel}
 \ker\lambda=J+J^*.$$ The inclusion from right to left is immediate. For the converse, let $\lambda(y)=0$ and put $\zeta=\pi(y)\xi$, which is orthogonal to $\xi$. If $\zeta=0$, then $y\in J$. Otherwise, on $K=\operatorname{span}\{\xi,\zeta\}$, define $R\xi=\zeta$ and $R\zeta=0$. The selfadjoint form of Kadison’s Transitivity Theorem <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[Kadison1957](#ref-Kadison1957)] supplies selfadjoint $h,g\in D$ whose restrictions to $K$ are respectively $\operatorname{Re}R$ and $\operatorname{Im}R$. Thus $v=h+ig$ satisfies $$\pi(v)\xi=\zeta,\qquad \pi(v^*)\xi=0.$$ Consequently $y-v\in J$ and $v\in J^*$, proving <a href="#norm:state-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:state-kernel">[norm:state-kernel]</a>.

Let $(e_\alpha)$ be a positive contractive approximate unit for $H$ and put $q_\alpha=1-e_\alpha$. Each $q_\alpha$ fixes $\xi$. For $z\in J$, the element $z^*z$ belongs to $H$, and hence $$\|zq_\alpha\|^2
   =\|q_\alpha z^*zq_\alpha\|\longrightarrow0.$$ Its adjoint gives $\|q_\alpha z^*\|\to0$. Applying <a href="#norm:state-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:state-kernel">[norm:state-kernel]</a> to $y-\lambda(y)1$ now gives $$\|q_\alpha(y-\lambda(y)1)q_\alpha\|\longrightarrow0
 \qquad(y\in D).$$ Choose one index sufficiently late for all the finitely many tests. ◻

</details>

We will apply excision to make finitely many word representatives almost orthonormal on one hereditary support. Their Gram matrix will control the norm of the row formed from these representatives. We also estimate how that row intertwines the action of each prescribed element below the outermost word level; a taper will later remove that boundary level.

To construct this matrix form of the compression, fix a finite set $F\subset A$ of selfadjoint contractions, $d\ge1$, and $0<\eta<1$. Suppose $\sigma\in\mathcal K(x,\gamma)$, where $x$ is a positive contraction. Choose a unit vector $\xi$ with $$\lambda(x):=\langle\sigma(x)\xi,\xi\rangle>\gamma/2.$$ Every nonzero vector in an irreducible representation is cyclic, so this is a pure state of $\widetilde A$. Let $\mathcal H_l$ be the span of the vectors $\sigma(w)\xi$, where $w$ is a word of length at most $l$ in $F$, with the empty word equal to $1$. These finite-dimensional spaces form an increasing filtration $$\mathbb C\xi=\mathcal H_0\subseteq\mathcal H_1
       \subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathcal H_d.$$ Choose an orthonormal basis $\xi_1,\ldots,\xi_m$ adapted to this filtration, with $\xi_1=\xi$. Write $\xi_j=\sigma(b_j)\xi$, where $b_j\in\widetilde A$ is a finite linear combination of the appropriate words; take $b_1=1$. Let $l_j\in\{0,\ldots,d\}$ be the level of $\xi_j$. There is exactly one level-zero vector.

<a id="norm:banded-model"></a>
For $a\in F$, let $M_a\in M_m$ be the matrix of its compression to $\mathcal H_d$ in this basis. It is a selfadjoint contraction. Moreover, $$\label{norm:banded-model}
 (M_a)_{ij}=0\quad\text{if }|l_i-l_j|>1.$$ Indeed, $a\mathcal H_l\subseteq\mathcal H_{l+1}$ for $l<d$; orthogonality to the filtration proves one half of the assertion, and selfadjointness proves the other. For $l_j<d$, put $$r_j(a)=ab_j-\sum_{l=1}^m b_l(M_a)_{lj}.$$ Since $a\xi_j\in\mathcal H_d$, these residuals satisfy $\sigma(r_j(a))\xi=0$.

<a id="stmt-norm-prototype"></a>
<div id="norm:prototype" class="lemma">

<a id="norm:positive-compression"></a>
**Lemma 25**. *For this finite word model there is a positive contractive seed $s\in A$ with $\sigma(s)\ne0$, whose support $P=p_s$ satisfies <a id="norm:local-gram"></a><a id="norm:local-residual"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 PxP&\ge(\gamma/4)P,                                      \label{norm:positive-compression}\\
 \big\|(Pb_i^*b_jP)_{i,j=1}^m-I_m\otimes P\big\|
      &\le\eta^2/4,                                     \label{norm:local-gram}\\
 \big\|(Pr_i(a)^*r_j(a)P)_{l_i,l_j<d}\big\|
      &\le\eta^2/4\qquad(a\in F).                        \label{norm:local-residual}
\end{aligned}$$ Every positive element in $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(s)$ has a support on which the same three estimates hold.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-norm-prototype"><summary>Proof of stmt-norm-prototype</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Lemma <a href="#norm:excision" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:excision">24</a> in $\widetilde A$ to $x$, all $b_i^*b_j$, and all $r_i(a)^*r_j(a)$. Their state values are respectively $\lambda(x)>\gamma/2$, $\delta_{ij}$, and zero. Choose the excision error at most $\gamma/16$ for $x$, and at most $\eta^2/(16m)$ for each of the matrix entries. Put $$P_0=1_{(1/2,1]}(q),\qquad v=q^{-1}P_0
 \quad\text{in }\widetilde A^{**}.$$ Here $\|v\|\le2$, and $$P_0(y-\lambda(y)1)P_0
       =v\big(qyq-\lambda(y)q^2\big)v.$$ The estimate for $x$ therefore gives $P_0xP_0\ge(\lambda(x)-\gamma/4)P_0\ge(\gamma/4)P_0$. For the matrices, each entry has error at most $\eta^2/(4m)$. An $r$-by-$r$ operator matrix whose entries have norm at most $\delta$ has norm at most $r\delta$, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for its row action. Since each matrix here has size at most $m$, both matrix errors are at most $\eta^2/4$.

Choose a continuous function $f:[0,1]\to[0,1]$ that is zero on $[0,1/2]$ and has $f(1)=1$, and set $s=f(q)xf(q)$. This is a positive contraction in $A$, because $A$ is an ideal in its unitization. Also $\lambda(s)=\lambda(x)>0$, so $\sigma(s)\ne0$, and $p_s\le P_0$. Compressing the preceding inequalities to $p_s$ proves the claims. Further hereditary restriction only decreases the support projection, so preserves all the estimates. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Separating the models

The next observation records precisely why iterating Lemma <a href="#lem:shrinking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:shrinking">20</a> does not lose the representation cover.

<a id="stmt-norm-separated-cover"></a>
<div id="norm:separated-cover" class="lemma">

<a id="norm:retained-cover"></a>
<a id="norm:cross-compressions"></a>
**Lemma 26**. *Assume that $A$ satisfies <span class="upright">(C)</span>. Suppose that positive contractions $s_1,\ldots,s_m\in A$ have nonvanishing loci covering $\mathcal K(x,\gamma)$. For each pair $i\ne j$, prescribe a finite set $\mathcal E_{ij}\subset\widetilde A$, and let $\delta>0$. There are positive contractions $t_i\in\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(s_i)$, with supports $Q_i=p_{t_i}$, such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \max_i\|\sigma(t_i)\|&=1
                 &&(\sigma\in\mathcal K(x,\gamma)),       \label{norm:retained-cover}\\
 \|Q_i z Q_j\|&\le\delta
                 &&(i\ne j,\ z\in\mathcal E_{ij}).        \label{norm:cross-compressions}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-norm-separated-cover"><summary>Proof of stmt-norm-separated-cover</summary>

*Proof.* If $\mathcal K(x,\gamma)$ is empty, take every $t_i=0$. Otherwise proceed as follows. Let $c<1$ be the constant in Lemma <a href="#lem:shrinking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:shrinking">20</a>. Before each shrinking operation, apply the last assertion of Lemma <a href="#norm:finite-cover" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:finite-cover">23</a> to the current finite family. This leaves every support unchanged and ensures that each representation in $\mathcal K(x,\gamma)$ gives norm one to at least one of the current seeds.

For one prescribed triple $i,j,z$, let $\beta=\|p_{s_i}zp_{s_j}\|$, using the current seeds. If $\beta=0$, nothing is needed. Otherwise apply Lemma <a href="#lem:shrinking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:shrinking">20</a> to the current seeds $s_i,s_j$ and to $z/\beta$. Their replacements have supports on which the compression of $z$ has norm at most $c\beta$. For any $\sigma\in\mathcal K(x,\gamma)$, choose a current seed of represented norm one. If its index is outside $\{i,j\}$, it survives unchanged. If its index is in $\{i,j\}$, the representation clause in Lemma <a href="#lem:shrinking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:shrinking">20</a> ensures that at least one replacement is nonzero. Thus the new family still covers $\mathcal K(x,\gamma)$, allowing the next reparametrization.

All new supports are subprojections of their previous supports. Consequently no previously obtained compression bound can increase. For a fixed coefficient $z$, finitely many repetitions suffice: after $r$ repetitions its bound is at most $c^r\|z\|$. There are only finitely many prescribed coefficients and pairs. Treating them successively therefore gives <a href="#norm:cross-compressions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:cross-compressions">[norm:cross-compressions]</a>. A final support-preserving reparametrization gives <a href="#norm:retained-cover" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:retained-cover">[norm:retained-cover]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### The row map and the taper

<a id="stmt-norm-finite-stage"></a>
<div id="norm:finite-stage" class="lemma">

<a id="norm:order-zero-error"></a>
<a id="norm:central-error"></a>
<a id="norm:finite-visibility"></a>
**Lemma 27**. *Assume that $A$ satisfies <span class="upright">(C)</span>. Let $x\in A$ be a positive contraction, $\gamma>0$, $k\ge2$, $F\subset A$ a finite set of selfadjoint contractions, $d\ge1$, and $0<\eta<1$. There is a c.p.c. map $V:M_k\to A$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \|V(z)V(w)\|&\le\eta^2
   &&(z,w\in(M_k)_+,\ \|z\|,\|w\|\le1,\ zw=0),            \label{norm:order-zero-error}\\
 \|[a,V(z)]\|&\le2\eta+2/d
   &&(a\in F,\ \|z\|\le1),                               \label{norm:central-error}\\
 \|\sigma(xV(1))\|&\ge\gamma^2/32
   &&(\sigma\in\mathcal K(x,\gamma)).                    \label{norm:finite-visibility}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-norm-finite-stage"><summary>Proof of stmt-norm-finite-stage</summary>

*Proof.* If $\mathcal K(x,\gamma)$ is empty, take $V=0$. Otherwise construct the preceding word model and the seed in Lemma <a href="#norm:prototype" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:prototype">25</a> for each representation in that set. The seeds cover the set. Lemma <a href="#norm:finite-cover" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:finite-cover">23</a> therefore retains finitely many models, indexed by $i=1,\ldots,m$. Write $n_i$ for their basis sizes, $b_j^{(i)}$ for their basis representatives, $l_j^{(i)}$ for their levels, and $M_a^{(i)}$ and $r_j^{(i)}(a)$ for their model matrices and residuals. Set $N=\sum_i n_i$.

Apply Lemma <a href="#norm:separated-cover" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:separated-cover">26</a> with $\delta=\eta^2/(2N)$ to all coefficients $$(b_j^{(i)})^*b_l^{(h)}
 \quad\text{and}\quad
 (r_j^{(i)}(a))^*r_l^{(h)}(a)
 \qquad(i\ne h,\ a\in F),$$ where the residual indices have levels less than $d$. Let $s_i$ denote the resulting seeds and $P_i=p_{s_i}$. All the estimates of Lemma <a href="#norm:prototype" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:prototype">25</a> remain valid. The number of models and their sizes have already been fixed before this cross-compression tolerance is chosen.

<a id="norm:cone-norm-one"></a>
<a id="norm:row-gram"></a>
By Lemma <a href="#lem:placement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:placement">17</a>, choose c.p.c. order-zero maps $\phi_i:M_k\to\mathop{\mathrm{her}}(s_i)$ such that $$\label{norm:cone-norm-one}
 \|\sigma(s_i)\|=1\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \|\sigma(\phi_i(1))\|=1.$$ In fact it suffices to apply that Lemma to the fixed cut $(s_i-1/2)_+$. In the universal representation on $\mathcal H$, consider $$\mathcal L=\bigoplus_{i=1}^m\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n_i}P_i\mathcal H
 \quad\text{and}\quad
 B:\mathcal L\to\mathcal H,\qquad
 B(\zeta_{ij})=\sum_{i,j}b_j^{(i)}\zeta_{ij}.$$ The row $B$ is only an operator on these support spaces. Its Gram matrix has $(ij,hl)$-entry $P_i(b_j^{(i)})^*b_l^{(h)}P_h$. The diagonal blocks differ from the corresponding identity blocks by at most $\eta^2/4$. The off-diagonal matrix has $N$ rows and columns with entries bounded by $\delta$, so has norm at most $N\delta=\eta^2/2$. In particular, $$\label{norm:row-gram}
 \|B^*B-1_{\mathcal L}\|\le\eta^2,\qquad
 \|B\|\le(1+\eta^2)^{1/2}.$$

<a id="norm:row-residual"></a>
Let $$M_a=\bigoplus_i(M_a^{(i)}\otimes1_{P_i\mathcal H})
       \quad\text{on }\mathcal L,$$ and let $Q$ be the projection onto the slots of level less than $d$. The columns of $(aB-BM_a)Q$ are $r_j^{(i)}(a)P_i$. Its squared norm is the norm of their Gram matrix. The local residual estimates and the same off-diagonal estimate therefore give $$\label{norm:row-residual}
 \|(aB-BM_a)Q\|\le\eta\qquad(a\in F).$$

<a id="norm:row-map"></a>
For $z\in M_k$, define operators on $\mathcal L$ by $$D_z=\bigoplus_{i,j}\phi_i(z)|_{P_i\mathcal H},\qquad
 T=\bigoplus_{i,j}(1-l_j^{(i)}/d)1_{P_i\mathcal H},\qquad
 C_z=TD_z .$$ The map $z\mapsto C_z$ is c.p.c. and order zero: it is the direct sum of the order-zero maps $\phi_i$, each multiplied by a nonnegative scalar at most one. It is supported on $Q$, since the taper $T$ vanishes at level $d$. Put $$\label{norm:row-map}
 \lambda_0=(1+\eta^2)^{-1},\qquad
 V(z)=\lambda_0 BC_zB^*
     =\lambda_0\sum_{i,j}(1-l_j^{(i)}/d)
                    b_j^{(i)}\phi_i(z)(b_j^{(i)})^* .$$ The last formula shows that $V(z)\in A$, including when $A$ is nonunital. Row compression gives complete positivity. Furthermore $0\le C_1\le1_{\mathcal L}$, so $\|V(1)\|\le\lambda_0\|B\|^2\le1$; hence $V$ is contractive.

For orthogonal positive contractions $z,w\in M_k$, we have $C_zC_w=0$. Thus $$V(z)V(w)
   =\lambda_0^2 BC_z(B^*B-1_{\mathcal L})C_wB^*,$$ and <a href="#norm:row-gram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:row-gram">[norm:row-gram]</a> gives $$\|V(z)V(w)\|
       \le\lambda_0^2(1+\eta^2)\eta^2
       \le\eta^2 .$$ This proves <a href="#norm:order-zero-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:order-zero-error">[norm:order-zero-error]</a>.

We next bound the commutator. Let $E_l$ be the projection onto all slots of level $l$, summed over the prototypes. By <a href="#norm:banded-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:banded-model">[norm:banded-model]</a>, the selfadjoint contraction $M_a$ has only its level diagonal and its two adjacent-level diagonals. The operators $$M_{a,+}=\sum_{l=0}^{d-1}E_{l+1}M_aE_l,\qquad
 M_{a,-}=M_{a,+}^*$$ each have norm at most one: their separate summands have orthogonal initial spaces and orthogonal final spaces. The level diagonal commutes with $T$, and $$[M_a,T]=d^{-1}(M_{a,+}-M_{a,-}),\qquad
 \|[M_a,T]\|\le2/d .$$ Within each prototype the entries of $M_a$ are scalar and the operator $\phi_i(z)$ is the same in every slot. Thus $[M_a,D_z]=0$, and <a id="norm:taper-bound"></a>$$\label{norm:taper-bound}
 \|[M_a,C_z]\|\le2/d\qquad(\|z\|\le1).$$ With $R_a=aB-BM_a$, selfadjointness gives $R_a^*=B^*a-M_aB^*$, and a direct expansion yields $$[a,V(z)]
 =\lambda_0\big(R_aC_zB^*+
                 B[M_a,C_z]B^*-BC_zR_a^*\big).$$ Because $C_z=QC_zQ$, <a href="#norm:row-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:row-residual">[norm:row-residual]</a> bounds both outside error terms. Consequently $$\|[a,V(z)]\|
 \le\lambda_0\big(2\eta\|B\|+(2/d)\|B\|^2\big)
 \le2\eta+2/d .$$ This proves <a href="#norm:central-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:central-error">[norm:central-error]</a>; the estimate is uniform over the unit ball of $M_k$.

Finally fix $\sigma\in\mathcal K(x,\gamma)$. The retained cover gives an index $i$ with $\|\sigma(s_i)\|=1$, so that $h_i=\sigma(\phi_i(1))$ has norm one by <a href="#norm:cone-norm-one" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:cone-norm-one">[norm:cone-norm-one]</a>. Denote the represented support by $P_i^\sigma$, and put $X=\sigma(x)$, $H=\sigma(V(1))$. The unique level-zero slot of the $i$-th model has representative $b_1^{(i)}=1$ and taper coefficient one. Every summand of <a href="#norm:row-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:row-map">[norm:row-map]</a> at $z=1$ is positive, so $H\ge\lambda_0 h_i$. Also $$P_i^\sigma X^2P_i^\sigma
 \ge(P_i^\sigma XP_i^\sigma)^2
 \ge(\gamma/4)^2P_i^\sigma,$$ where the first inequality follows by expanding $P_i^\sigma X(1-P_i^\sigma)XP_i^\sigma\ge0$, and the second uses <a href="#norm:positive-compression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:positive-compression">[norm:positive-compression]</a>. Since $h_i=P_i^\sigma h_iP_i^\sigma$, $$\|Xh_iX\|
  =\|h_i^{1/2}X^2h_i^{1/2}\|
  \ge(\gamma/4)^2\|h_i\|
  =(\gamma/4)^2 .$$ Positive domination and $\|X\|\le1$ now imply $$\|XH\|\ge\|XHX\|
       \ge\lambda_0\|Xh_iX\|
       \ge\gamma^2/32,$$ because $\eta<1$ gives $\lambda_0\ge1/2$. This proves <a href="#norm:finite-visibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:finite-visibility">[norm:finite-visibility]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-norm-finite-stage-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-norm-finite-stage</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:norm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:norm">22</a>.* Choose increasing finite sets $F_n$ of selfadjoint contractions whose union is dense in the selfadjoint unit ball of $A$. Apply Lemma <a href="#norm:finite-stage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:finite-stage">27</a> with $F=F_n$, $d=n$, and $\eta=(n+1)^{-1}$, and denote its maps by $\nu_n$. Complete positivity and contractivity pass to the ultrapower. The error in <a href="#norm:order-zero-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:order-zero-error">[norm:order-zero-error]</a> tends to zero, so the resulting map is order zero. The estimate in <a href="#norm:central-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="norm:central-error">[norm:central-error]</a>, density, and $\|\nu_n(z)\|\le\|z\|$ imply that its range commutes with $A$. All maps have norm at most one, which supplies the bound required to form their representing sequences. The final estimate in Lemma <a href="#norm:finite-stage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:finite-stage">27</a> holds for every $n$ and every $\sigma\in\mathcal K(x,\gamma)$, with the same constant $\gamma^2/32$. This proves the stated simultaneous coordinate quantifiers. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-norm-scope"></a>
<div id="norm:scope" class="remark">

*Remark 28*. Proposition <a href="#prop:norm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:norm">22</a> controls irreducible representations of the coordinate algebra $A$. It supplies the representation estimates needed for later ideal comparisons; it makes no assertion of fullness in $A_\omega\cap A'$ or in its annihilator quotient.

</div>

<a id="sec:tracial"></a>
### Matrix cones large for bounded traces

From this section onward, unless stated otherwise, $A$ is a separable nuclear $C^*$-algebra satisfying <span class="upright">(C)</span>.

The matrix cones in this section are approximately central in tracial $2$-norm. The construction uses a convex nuclear approximation, and its estimates are independent of the number of maps in that approximation. Throughout, $D\subseteq A$ is hereditary and $T_{\leq1}(D)$ denotes the positive bounded traces on $D$ of norm at most one. We extend these traces normally to $D^{**}$. On matrices we use the unnormalized extension $\mathcal T=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\otimes\tau$, and write $\|x\|_{2,\tau}=\mathcal T(x^*x)^{1/2}$, also for rectangular matrices. The traces need not be faithful. All estimates below hold simultaneously for every $\tau\in T_{\leq1}(D)$.

<a id="stmt-prop-bounded-trace"></a>
<div id="prop:bounded-trace" class="proposition">

<a id="tr:central-target"></a>
<a id="tr:mass-target"></a>
**Proposition 29** (Bounded-trace matrix cones). *Fix $k\geq2$ and put $$L=(20k)^2,\qquad C_k=1+4L.$$ Let $F\subset D$ be a finite set of selfadjoint contractions, let $\mathcal P\subseteq F$ consist of positive contractions, and let $0<\delta<1$. For every $\varepsilon>0$ there is a c.p.c. order-zero map $V:M_k\to D$ such that, for every $\tau\in T_{\leq1}(D)$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \sup_{\|x\|\leq1}\|[a,V(x)]\|_{2,\tau}
 &<\varepsilon &&(a\in F),\label{tr:central-target}\\
 \tau(aV(1))
 &\geq \frac\delta3\tau(a)-C_k\delta^{3/2}-\varepsilon
 &&(a\in\mathcal P).\label{tr:mass-target}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, $k$ is fixed before the accuracy is chosen; no bound on the number of nuclear approximation summands is required.*

</div>

We first record the functional-calculus estimate used to regularize a row whose operator norm need not be uniformly bounded.

<a id="stmt-tr-lipschitz"></a>
<div id="tr:lipschitz" class="lemma">

**Lemma 30** (Lipschitz calculus in tracial $2$-norm). *Let $\mathcal M$ be a von Neumann algebra with a finite normal trace $T$, let $H=H^*\in\mathcal M$, and let $B\in\mathcal M$. If $f$ is real-valued and Lipschitz on an interval containing the spectrum of $H$, then $$\|[f(H),B]\|_{2,T}
 \leq\operatorname{Lip}(f)\|[H,B]\|_{2,T}.$$ Consequently, if $W$ is a finite row, $a=a^*$ and $m=m^*$ are compatible contractions, and $$R=W(1+W^*W)^{-1/2},$$ then $\|R\|\leq1$ and $$\|aR-Rm\|_{2,T}\leq\|aW-Wm\|_{2,T}.$$ The constants do not depend on the size of the matrices or on $\|W\|$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tr-lipschitz"><summary>Proof of stmt-tr-lipschitz</summary>

*Proof.* For a finite spectral resolution $H=\sum_j\alpha_j e_j$, the blocks $e_iBe_j$ are orthogonal in $L^2(\mathcal M,T)$. Thus $$\begin{aligned}
 \|[f(H),B]\|_{2,T}^2
 &=\sum_{i,j}|f(\alpha_i)-f(\alpha_j)|^2\|e_iBe_j\|_{2,T}^2\\
 &\leq\operatorname{Lip}(f)^2
       \sum_{i,j}|\alpha_i-\alpha_j|^2\|e_iBe_j\|_{2,T}^2.
