\end{aligned}$$ Approximate an arbitrary $H$ in operator norm by finite spectral step functions of $H$. The corresponding functions $f(H)$ also converge in norm. Since $T(1)<\infty$, passage to $2$-norm proves the assertion. This passage is made separately in each finite matrix algebra; the resulting inequality has no factor involving its size or $T(1)$.

For the row assertion use $$H=\begin{pmatrix}0&W\\W^*&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 B=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\0&m\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 f(t)=\frac{t}{\sqrt{1+t^2}}.$$ This function has Lipschitz constant one, and $f(H)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&R\\R^*&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$. The squares of the two commutator norms in the preceding inequality are respectively $2\|aR-Rm\|_{2,T}^2$ and $2\|aW-Wm\|_{2,T}^2$. ◻

</details>

<a id="tr:commutant-corners"></a>
We shall also use the following description of a finite-dimensional commutant. If $h\in D_+$ and $\rho:E\to M(\mathop{\mathrm{her}}_D(h))$ is a unital homomorphism from a finite-dimensional algebra, with unit $p_h$ in $D^{**}$, write $E=\bigoplus_jM_{n_j}$ and let $v^{(j)}_{rs}$ be its represented matrix units. Then $$\label{tr:commutant-corners}
 \mathop{\mathrm{her}}_D(h)\cap\rho(E)'
 \cong\bigoplus_j v^{(j)}_{11}\mathop{\mathrm{her}}_D(h)v^{(j)}_{11},\qquad
 (b_j)_j\longmapsto\sum_{j,r}v^{(j)}_{r1}b_jv^{(j)}_{1r}.$$ Indeed, commuting with the diagonal matrix units kills every unwanted block, and commuting with the remaining matrix units identifies the retained diagonal blocks by the displayed formula. Each corner on the right is hereditary in $D$. Averaging over the compact unitary group of $E$ gives a trace-preserving conditional expectation $$\mathbb E_\rho:p_hD^{**}p_h\longrightarrow
                  p_hD^{**}p_h\cap\rho(E)'.$$ It preserves $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}_D(h)$ because the represented unitaries are multipliers. It is contractive in tracial $2$-norm, by the triangle inequality for the integral. If $b$ is in its range, then $\tau(b\mathbb E_\rho(x))=\tau(bx)$ whenever these expressions are defined. These observations allow us to apply Lemma <a href="#lem:placement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:placement">17</a> in the commutant, simultaneously to all its bounded positive traces.

<details id="proof-stmt-tr-lipschitz-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-tr-lipschitz</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:bounded-trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:bounded-trace">29</a>.* We will combine small matrix cones chosen in the commutants of the nuclear approximation maps. Their supports are chosen to make the model and overlap errors summable, after which a regularized row gives a contraction. A second support compression makes this row bounded below on the retained support. The polar isometry of the compressed row then transports an exact matrix cone to $D$ while retaining the tracial mass needed for the stated estimate.

Fix $F,\mathcal P,k,\delta$. Our approximation parameter is $0<u<1/4$. The notation $o_u(1)$ below always means a quantity tending to zero uniformly over $T_{\leq1}(D)$ with this fixed data. It has no dependence on the number of approximation summands.

**The convex approximation and its weighted errors.** The hereditary algebra $D$ is nuclear. The convex order-zero return approximation is due to Hirshberg, Kirchberg, and White <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[HKW2012](#ref-HKW2012), Theorem 1.4]; it is also part of the stronger Brown–Carrión–White approximation theorem <a id="pm-cite-50"></a>[[BCW2019](#ref-BCW2019), Theorem 3.1]. This supplies a finite-dimensional unital algebra $E$, one c.p.c. map $\alpha:D\to E$, positive numbers $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_r$ with $\sum_l\lambda_l=1$, and c.p.c.  order-zero maps $\beta_l:E\to D$, such that $$\Phi=\sum_{l=1}^r\lambda_l\beta_l\alpha$$ is as close to the identity as prescribed on a given finite set. The integer $r$ may depend on $u$ without restriction. Put $$h_l=\beta_l(1),\quad p_l=p_{h_l},\quad
 \beta_l(x)=h_l\rho_l(x),\quad
 \mu_l=\rho_l\alpha,\quad\theta_l=h_l\mu_l.$$ Here $\rho_l$ is the supporting homomorphism of the order-zero map <a id="pm-cite-51"></a>[[WZ2009](#ref-WZ2009)]; it acts by multipliers of $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}_D(h_l)$. In particular $h_l$ commutes with $\mu_l(D)$.

<a id="tr:mean-square"></a>
<a id="tr:unit-error"></a>
By approximating $F$, its squares, and a sufficiently late positive contractive approximate-unit element $e\in D$, we arrange $$\begin{aligned}
 \left\|\sum_l\lambda_l(a-\theta_l(a))^2\right\|
 &\leq u^4 &&(a\in F),\label{tr:mean-square}\\
 \left\|a^{1/2}\left(1-\sum_l\lambda_lh_l\right)a^{1/2}\right\|
 &\leq u^4 &&(a\in\mathcal P).\label{tr:unit-error}
\end{aligned}$$ For completeness, the Schwarz inequality gives $$0\leq\sum_l\lambda_l(a-\theta_l(a))^2
 \leq a^2-a\Phi(a)-\Phi(a)a+\Phi(a^2),$$ whose norm is at most $3v$ if both relevant approximation errors are at most $v$. For the second estimate choose $e$ so that $\|a^{1/2}(1-e)a^{1/2}\|\leq u^4/2$ for all targets, and approximate $e$ within $u^4/2$. Since $\theta_l(e)\leq h_l$, this proves <a href="#tr:unit-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:unit-error">[tr:unit-error]</a>. All appearances of $1$ in this proof are allowed in the bidual; no unit of $D$ is assumed.

<a id="tr:g-loss"></a>
<a id="tr:model-square"></a>
Choose the following explicit continuous cutoff on $[0,1]$: $$g(t)=\begin{cases}
 0,&t\leq1-2u,\\
 (t-1+2u)/u,&1-2u<t<1-u,\\
 1,&t\geq1-u.
 \end{cases}$$ Set $g_l=g(h_l)$ and $z_l=1_{(1-2u,1]}(h_l)$. Thus $p_{g_l}\leq z_l\leq p_l$, and the scalar inequality $$\label{tr:g-loss}
 1-g_l^4\leq u^{-1}(1-h_l)$$ holds by functional calculus. With $$D_l=\sum_{a\in F}p_l(a-\mu_l(a))^2p_l,\qquad
 B_u=2u^4+8u^2,$$ we have, for every $a\in F$, $$\label{tr:model-square}
 \sum_l\lambda_l\|(a-\mu_l(a))z_l\|_{2,\tau}^2\leq B_u,
 \qquad
 \sum_l\lambda_l\tau(z_lD_l)\leq |F|B_u.$$ Indeed, the contribution of $a-\theta_l(a)$ is bounded by $u^4$ using <a href="#tr:mean-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:mean-square">[tr:mean-square]</a> and the trace inequality $\tau(z_lxz_l)\leq\tau(x)$ for $x\geq0$. On $z_l$, $\|(\theta_l(a)-\mu_l(a))z_l\|\leq2u$. Apply $\|x+y\|_2^2\leq2\|x\|_2^2+2\|y\|_2^2$ and $\sum_l\lambda_l\tau(z_l)\leq1$.

<a id="tr:T1-lower"></a>
<a id="tr:T1-upper"></a>
Weighted Cauchy–Schwarz now gives, for $a\in\mathcal P$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_l\lambda_l\tau(\mu_l(a)g_l^4)
 &\geq\tau(a)-u^3-\sqrt{B_u},\label{tr:T1-lower}\\
 \sum_l\lambda_l\tau(\mu_l(a)z_l)
 &\leq\tau(a)+\sqrt{B_u}.\label{tr:T1-upper}
\end{aligned}$$ To verify the replacement of $\mu_l(a)$ by $a$, for example, $$\left|\sum_l\lambda_l\tau((\mu_l(a)-a)g_l^4)\right|
 \leq
 \left(\sum_l\lambda_l\|(\mu_l(a)-a)z_l\|_2^2\right)^{1/2}
 \left(\sum_l\lambda_l\tau(g_l^8)\right)^{1/2}
 \leq\sqrt{B_u}.$$ The same argument applies to $z_l$. The lost $a$-mass in the first inequality is at most $u^3$, by <a href="#tr:g-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:g-loss">[tr:g-loss]</a> and <a href="#tr:unit-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:unit-error">[tr:unit-error]</a>; in the second use $z_l\leq1$.

<a id="tr:first-pencil"></a>
**The first pencil: small supports and summable overlaps.** For each $l$ put $$C_l=\mathop{\mathrm{her}}_D(h_l)\cap\rho_l(E)',\qquad
 \mathbb E_l=\mathbb E_{\rho_l}.$$ Construct maps $\psi_l:M_k\to C_l$ inductively, writing $t_l=\psi_l(1)$. Once the earlier maps have been chosen, we penalize both the model error and overlap with their weights by setting $$\begin{aligned}
 S_l&=\sum_{i<l}t_i^2,\qquad
 K_l=\mathbb E_l\big(u^{-1}D_l+\delta^{-1/2}p_lS_lp_l\big),\nonumber\\
 X_l&=g_l(p_l-K_l)g_l,\qquad
 b_l=g_l(X_l)_+g_l.\label{tr:first-pencil}
\end{aligned}$$ These formulas define $b_l\in C_l$. To check membership before taking positive parts, sandwich each term of $D_l$ by $g_l$. Terms involving $a\in D$ then lie in $\mathop{\mathrm{her}}_D(h_l)$, and terms involving $\mu_l(a)$ lie there by multiplier action. The same is true of $g_lS_lg_l$. Averaging preserves this hereditary algebra and commutes with multiplication by $g_l$. Hence $X_l$ belongs to it and to the commutant, as does $b_l$.

<a id="tr:first-pencil-bounds"></a>
<a id="tr:first-support"></a>
The inequalities $X_l\leq p_l$ and $X_l\geq-(\|K_l\|+1)p_l$ imply $0\leq(X_l)_+\leq p_l$ by scalar functional calculus. Therefore $$\label{tr:first-pencil-bounds}
 0\leq b_l\leq g_l^2\leq z_l,\qquad
 b_l\geq g_l^4-g_l^2K_lg_l^2.$$ The second inequality follows from $(X_l)_+\geq X_l$. Lemma <a href="#lem:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:support">18</a>, applied to the pencil in <a href="#tr:first-pencil" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:first-pencil">[tr:first-pencil]</a>, gives $$\label{tr:first-support}
 p_{b_l}K_lp_{b_l}\leq p_{b_l}.$$

<a id="tr:placement"></a>
<a id="tr:support-budget"></a>
<a id="tr:T2-model"></a>
Use the tracial conclusion of Lemma <a href="#lem:placement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:placement">17</a> in $C_l$, which has the form <a href="#tr:commutant-corners" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:commutant-corners">[tr:commutant-corners]</a>, with $q=\delta\lambda_l$ and positive contraction $b_l$. It gives a c.p.c. order-zero map $\psi_l:M_k\to\mathop{\mathrm{her}}_{C_l}(b_l)$ whose weight $t_l$ satisfies $$\label{tr:placement}
 d_\sigma(t_l)\leq\delta\lambda_l d_\sigma(b_l),\qquad
 d_\sigma((t_l-1/2)_+)
 \geq\frac{\delta\lambda_l}{3}d_\sigma((b_l-1/2)_+)$$ for every bounded positive trace $\sigma$ on $C_l$. In particular, on restricting $\tau$ to $C_l$, $$\label{tr:support-budget}
 \tau(p_{t_l})\leq\delta\lambda_l,\qquad
 \sum_l\tau(p_{t_l})\leq\delta,\qquad \tau(S_l)\leq\delta.$$ Here ranks are the traces of the corresponding support projections in the bidual. As $p_{t_l}\leq p_{b_l}$, <a href="#tr:first-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:first-support">[tr:first-support]</a> gives $$\sum_l\tau(t_l^2K_l)\leq\sum_l\tau(t_l^2)\leq\delta.$$ We may remove $\mathbb E_l$ inside these traces because $t_l^2\in C_l$. Both summands defining $K_l$ are positive. It follows that <a id="tr:T2-overlap"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_l\|(a-\mu_l(a))t_l\|_{2,\tau}^2
 &\leq u\delta &&(a\in F),\label{tr:T2-model}\\
 \sum_l\tau(t_l^2S_l)&\leq\delta^{3/2}.
 \label{tr:T2-overlap}
\end{aligned}$$ These are unweighted sums: the individual support allowances $\delta\lambda_l$ in <a href="#tr:support-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:support-budget">[tr:support-budget]</a> have absorbed the convex coefficients. Their total is $\delta$, so increasing the number of approximation summands does not enlarge either error bound.

<a id="tr:cut-lower"></a>
<a id="tr:T3"></a>
We next obtain a lower bound for their high spectral cuts. Let $$s_l=1_{(1/2,1]}(t_l),\qquad e_l=1_{(1/2,1]}(b_l).$$ Since $b_l$ is a contraction supported under $z_l$, scalar functional calculus and <a href="#tr:first-pencil-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:first-pencil-bounds">[tr:first-pencil-bounds]</a> give $$\label{tr:cut-lower}
 e_l\geq2b_l-z_l\geq2g_l^4-2g_l^2K_lg_l^2-z_l.$$ For $a\in\mathcal P$, the functional $$\sigma_{l,a}(c)=\tau(\mu_l(a)c)\qquad(c\in C_l)$$ is a bounded positive trace. Indeed $\mu_l(a)$ is a positive contraction commuting with $C_l$, so positivity and the trace identity follow from those of $\tau$. Normality shows that $d_{\sigma_{l,a}}(t_l)=\tau(\mu_l(a)p_{t_l})$, and gives the same formula for every positive spectral cut. Thus <a href="#tr:placement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:placement">[tr:placement]</a> applies to this single weighted trace and yields $\tau(\mu_l(a)s_l)\geq(\delta\lambda_l/3)\tau(\mu_l(a)e_l)$. Using <a href="#tr:cut-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:cut-lower">[tr:cut-lower]</a> and $0\leq\mu_l(a)\leq1$, we obtain <a id="tr:weighted-mass"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_l\tau(\mu_l(a)s_l)
 &\geq \frac\delta3\sum_l\lambda_l
               \tau(\mu_l(a)(2g_l^4-z_l))
       -\frac{2\delta}{3}\sum_l\lambda_l\tau(g_l^2K_lg_l^2).
 \label{tr:weighted-mass}
\end{aligned}$$ For the last term, remove the expectation against $g_l^4\in C_l$: $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_l\lambda_l\tau(g_l^2K_lg_l^2)
 &=\sum_l\lambda_l\left(u^{-1}\tau(g_l^4D_l)
                   +\delta^{-1/2}\tau(g_l^4S_l)\right)\\
 &\leq u^{-1}|F|B_u+\sqrt\delta.
\end{aligned}$$ In the inequality use $g_l^4\leq z_l$, <a href="#tr:model-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:model-square">[tr:model-square]</a>, and $\tau(S_l)\leq\delta$. Combining this with <a href="#tr:T1-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:T1-lower">[tr:T1-lower]</a>–<a href="#tr:T1-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:T1-upper">[tr:T1-upper]</a> proves $$\label{tr:T3}
 \sum_l\tau(\mu_l(a)s_l)
 \geq\frac\delta3\tau(a)-\delta^{3/2}-r_u
 \qquad(a\in\mathcal P),$$ where one may take the explicit error $$r_u=\frac\delta3\left(2u^3+3\sqrt{B_u}
                          +2|F|B_u/u\right)\longrightarrow0.$$

<a id="tr:regularized-central"></a>
<a id="tr:regularized-overlap"></a>
**Regularizing the combined row.** Form matrices over $D$ and models in its bidual as follows: $$W=(t_1,\ldots,t_r),\quad D_0=\operatorname{diag}(t_l^2),\quad
 m(a)=\operatorname{diag}(\mu_l(a)),\quad p=p_{D_0},$$ $$R=W(1+W^*W)^{-1/2},\qquad Q_0=R^*R,\qquad
 G_0=D_0(1+D_0)^{-1}.$$ Here $\mathcal T(p)\leq\delta$ by <a href="#tr:support-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:support-budget">[tr:support-budget]</a>. Each $t_l$ commutes with $\mu_l(a)$, so <a href="#tr:T2-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:T2-model">[tr:T2-model]</a> says $$\|aW-Wm(a)\|_{2,\tau}\leq\sqrt{u\delta}=:e_u.$$ The diagonal of $W^*W-D_0$ is zero, and its $(i,j)$ entry off the diagonal is $t_it_j$. Consequently <a id="tr:off-diagonal-cost"></a>$$\label{tr:off-diagonal-cost}
 \|W^*W-D_0\|_{2,\tau}^2
 =2\sum_l\sum_{i<l}\tau(t_l^2t_i^2)
 =2\sum_l\tau(t_l^2S_l)\leq2\delta^{3/2}.$$ Lemma <a href="#tr:lipschitz" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:lipschitz">30</a> gives the first of the following estimates; the second follows by expanding the commutator of $R^*R$: $$\begin{aligned}
 \|aR-Rm(a)\|_{2,\tau}&\leq e_u,
 &\|[Q_0,m(a)]\|_{2,\tau}&\leq2e_u.
 \label{tr:regularized-central}
\end{aligned}$$ Finally, $Q_0=1-(1+W^*W)^{-1}$ and $G_0=1-(1+D_0)^{-1}$. The resolvent identity and the contractivity of both resolvents show that $$\label{tr:regularized-overlap}
 \|Q_0-G_0\|_{2,\tau}^2\leq2\delta^{3/2}.$$ This regularization has removed every occurrence of $\|W\|$ from the estimates.

**The second pencil in one matrix commutant.** Write $B_0=\mathop{\mathrm{her}}_{M_r(D)}(D_0)$. Both $W^*W$ and $D_0$ belong to $B_0$: for the former, use its entries $t_it_j$ and an approximate unit obtained by functional calculus of $D_0$. Hence $Q_0,G_0\in B_0$. Let $$\pi:M_k\longrightarrow M(B_0)$$ be the diagonal of the supporting homomorphisms of $\psi_l$, whose unit in the bidual is $p$. Its values commute with $\operatorname{diag}(t_l)$ and with $m(a)$. To see the latter assertion, recover each supporting homomorphism by bounded strong limits from $t_l$ and $\psi_l(M_k)$, all of which commute with $\mu_l(a)$. The multiplier assertion follows because its products with $\operatorname{diag}(t_l)$ belong to $M_r(D)$; the same observation shows that $m(a)$ acts by multipliers on $B_0$ and commutes with $p$.

The row $R$ is contractive, but its Gram operator $Q_0$ need not commute with $\pi(M_k)$, so $R\pi(\cdot)R^*$ need not have order zero. We use the small overlap cost in <a href="#tr:regularized-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:regularized-overlap">[tr:regularized-overlap]</a> to find a $\pi(M_k)$-invariant support on which $R$ is bounded below. The polar isometry of the compressed row will then transport the matrix action while the estimates below control the lost tracial mass.

<a id="tr:finite-average-bound"></a>
<a id="tr:second-cost"></a>
Let $u_k,v_k\in M_k$ be the clock and shift unitaries. The map $$\mathbb E_\pi(X)=\frac1{k^2}\sum_{i,j=0}^{k-1}
       \pi(u_k^iv_k^j)X\pi(u_k^iv_k^j)^*$$ is the trace-preserving expectation onto the commutant of $\pi(M_k)$ in the corner with unit $p$. The usual matrix-unit calculation shows that the average kills the nonconstant matrix coefficients, and fixes the commutant. Since the identity conjugation is one of its $k^2$ positive summands, $$\label{tr:finite-average-bound}
 X\leq k^2\mathbb E_\pi(X)\qquad(X\geq0).$$ Put $$K=\mathbb E_\pi((Q_0-G_0)^2).$$ Then $K\in B_0\cap\pi(M_k)'$, and $$\label{tr:second-cost}
 \mathcal T(K)\leq2\delta^{3/2},\qquad
 \|[m(a),K]\|_{2,\tau}\leq4e_u.$$ For the commutator use $[m(a),G_0]=0$, $\|Q_0-G_0\|\leq1$, <a href="#tr:regularized-central" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:regularized-central">[tr:regularized-central]</a>, and the $2$-norm contractivity of the expectation, which commutes with multiplication by $m(a)$.

<a id="tr:second-pencil"></a>
<a id="tr:second-pencil-bounds"></a>
<a id="tr:polar-lower"></a>
<a id="tr:c-commutator"></a>
Choose the fixed piecewise linear cutoff $$g'(t)=\begin{cases}
 0,&t\leq1/10,\\
 10t-1,&1/10<t<1/5,\\
 1,&t\geq1/5,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad g'=g'(G_0),$$ and, with $L=(20k)^2$, set $$\label{tr:second-pencil}
 X'=g'(p-LK)g',\qquad c=g'(X')_+g'.$$ As for the first pencil, $c\in B_0\cap\pi(M_k)'$ and $$\label{tr:second-pencil-bounds}
 0\leq c\leq(g')^2\leq p,\qquad
 c\geq(g')^4-L(g')^2K(g')^2,
 \qquad p_c\leq1_{(1/10,\infty)}(G_0).$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:support">18</a> gives $p_cKp_c\leq L^{-1}p_c$. For $\xi\in p_c\mathcal H$, <a href="#tr:finite-average-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:finite-average-bound">[tr:finite-average-bound]</a> implies $$\|(Q_0-G_0)\xi\|^2
 \leq k^2\langle K\xi,\xi\rangle
 \leq\frac1{400}\|\xi\|^2.$$ On the same space $G_0$ has compression at least $1/10$. Therefore $$\label{tr:polar-lower}
 c^2\geq cQ_0c\geq\frac1{20}c^2.$$ Furthermore $m(a)$ commutes with $g'$. Lemma <a href="#tr:lipschitz" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:lipschitz">30</a>, applied to the scalar function $t\mapsto t_+$ in <a href="#tr:second-pencil" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:second-pencil">[tr:second-pencil]</a>, yields the useful explicit bound $$\label{tr:c-commutator}
 \|[m(a),c]\|_{2,\tau}
 \leq L\|[m(a),K]\|_{2,\tau}\leq4Le_u.$$

<a id="tr:corrected-mass"></a>
There remains a lower bound on the mass surviving this second pencil. Set $s'=\operatorname{diag}(s_l)$. On $s_l$ one has $t_l>1/2$, so $t_l^2/(1+t_l^2)>1/5$, and hence $g's'=s'$. The scalar inequality $c^2\geq2c-p$, followed by <a href="#tr:second-pencil-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:second-pencil-bounds">[tr:second-pencil-bounds]</a>, gives $$s'c^2s'\geq2s'cs'-s'\geq s'-2Ls'Ks'.$$ For $a\in\mathcal P$, $m(a)$ is a positive contraction commuting with $s'$. Decomposing the trace along $s'$ and $p-s'$ shows that $\mathcal T(m(a)c^2)\geq\mathcal T(m(a)s'c^2s')$; the omitted diagonal contribution is nonnegative. Thus $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal T(m(a)c^2)
 &\geq\mathcal T(m(a)s')-2L\mathcal T(K)\nonumber\\
 &\geq\frac\delta3\tau(a)-(1+4L)\delta^{3/2}-r_u.
 \label{tr:corrected-mass}
\end{aligned}$$ Here we used <a href="#tr:T3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:T3">[tr:T3]</a> and <a href="#tr:second-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:second-cost">[tr:second-cost]</a>. The argument only uses the commutation of $m(a)$ with $s'$; it does not require $c$ to commute with $s'$.

<a id="tr:polar-intertwining"></a>
**Polar correction and exact order zero.** Put $d=cQ_0c$ and let $U$ be the polar partial isometry of the row $Rc$ in the bidual. Equation <a href="#tr:polar-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:polar-lower">[tr:polar-lower]</a> implies $p_d=p_c$, so $U^*U=p_c$. We claim that the row $$Y=Uc$$ belongs to $M_{1,r}(D)$ and satisfies $$\label{tr:polar-intertwining}
 \sup_{\tau\in T_{\leq1}(D)}\|aY-Ym(a)\|_{2,\tau}=o_u(1)
 \qquad(a\in F).$$ We give uniform estimates for this passage, since $r$ can grow with $u$.

<a id="tr:uniform-polar-norm"></a>
<a id="tr:uniform-polar-two"></a>
For $\eta>0$ define the algebraic row $$Y_\eta=Rc(d+\eta)^{-1/2}c\in M_{1,r}(D).$$ The domination $c^2\leq20d$ implies a factorization $c=d^{1/2}x$ with $\|x\|\leq\sqrt{20}$. One elementary proof is to define $x^*$ initially on the range of $d^{1/2}$ by $x^*(d^{1/2}\xi)=c\xi$; the stated domination makes this well-defined and bounded, and it extends by zero on the orthogonal complement. Using $Rc=Ud^{1/2}$ now gives $$\label{tr:uniform-polar-norm}
 \|Y-Y_\eta\|
 \leq\sqrt{20}\sup_{t\geq0}
        \sqrt t\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{t}{t+\eta}}\right)
 \leq\sqrt{20\eta}.$$ For the last scalar bound split at $t=\eta$: below it use $\sqrt t$, and above it use $1-\sqrt{t/(t+\eta)}\leq\eta/t$. In particular the norm limit $Y$ lies in the rectangular algebra. Both $Y$ and $Y_\eta$ have right support under $p$, so $$\label{tr:uniform-polar-two}
 \|Y-Y_\eta\|_{2,\tau}\leq\sqrt{20\eta\delta}.$$ This uses $\mathcal T(p)\leq\delta$, rather than a bound on the trace of the full matrix unit.

At fixed $\eta$ the intertwining error for $Y_\eta$ tends to zero uniformly with $u$. To spell this out, <a href="#tr:regularized-central" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:regularized-central">[tr:regularized-central]</a> and <a href="#tr:c-commutator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:c-commutator">[tr:c-commutator]</a> imply $$\|[m(a),d]\|_{2,\tau}\leq(8L+2)e_u.$$ On $[0,1]$ the function $(t+\eta)^{-1/2}$ has norm at most $\eta^{-1/2}$ and Lipschitz constant at most $\tfrac12\eta^{-3/2}$. Expand $aY_\eta-Y_\eta m(a)$ through its four factors and apply Lemma <a href="#tr:lipschitz" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:lipschitz">30</a>. This gives $$\|aY_\eta-Y_\eta m(a)\|_{2,\tau}
 \leq\left((1+8L)\eta^{-1/2}
                    +(4L+1)\eta^{-3/2}\right)e_u.$$ Together with <a href="#tr:uniform-polar-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:uniform-polar-two">[tr:uniform-polar-two]</a>, this bounds the error for $Y$ by the displayed right-hand side plus $2\sqrt{20\eta\delta}$. First choose $\eta$ small and then $u$ small. This proves <a href="#tr:polar-intertwining" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:polar-intertwining">[tr:polar-intertwining]</a> without any restriction on $r$.

Define $$V(x)=Uc\pi(x)cU^*=Y\pi(x)Y^*\qquad(x\in M_k).$$ These elements lie in $D$: the rows $Y_\eta\pi(x)$ belong to $M_{1,r}(D)$ by multiplier action, and converge in norm to $Y\pi(x)$ by <a href="#tr:uniform-polar-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:uniform-polar-norm">[tr:uniform-polar-norm]</a>. The map is completely positive and contractive, since $V(1)=Uc^2U^*\leq1$. It is exactly order zero. Indeed $c$ commutes with $\pi(M_k)$, $U^*U=p_c$, and for orthogonal positive $x,y\in M_k$, $$V(x)V(y)=Uc^4\pi(xy)U^*=0.$$ For a selfadjoint $a\in F$ set $\Delta_a=aY-Ym(a)$. Since $m(a)$ commutes with $\pi(M_k)$ and $\|Y\|\leq1$, $$[a,V(x)]=\Delta_a\pi(x)Y^*-Y\pi(x)\Delta_a^*,\qquad
 \|[a,V(x)]\|_{2,\tau}\leq2\|x\|\|\Delta_a\|_{2,\tau}.$$ This proves <a href="#tr:central-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:central-target">[tr:central-target]</a> when $u$ is sufficiently small. Finally $Y^*Y=c^2$ and $\|Y\|_{2,\tau}\leq\sqrt\delta$. Rectangular traciality and Cauchy–Schwarz give $$\left|\tau(aV(1))-\mathcal T(m(a)c^2)\right|
 =\left|\mathcal T(Y^*\Delta_a)\right|
 \leq\sqrt\delta\,\|\Delta_a\|_{2,\tau}=o_u(1).$$ Combining this with <a href="#tr:corrected-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:corrected-mass">[tr:corrected-mass]</a>, and choosing $u$ to make $r_u+\sqrt\delta\sup_\tau\|\Delta_a\|_{2,\tau}<\varepsilon$ for the finitely many targets, proves <a href="#tr:mass-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:mass-target">[tr:mass-target]</a>. All choices were made for the entire family $T_{\leq1}(D)$ at once, completing the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:localization"></a>
### Joining matrix cones and localizing traces

Our goal is to retain the tracial mass from the previous section while obtaining norm-central maps. The trace-kernel ideal and relative commutant lifting methods have predecessors in Kirchberg–Rørdam <a id="pm-cite-52"></a>[[KirchbergRordam2014](#ref-KirchbergRordam2014), Section 4]. Here the relative ideal is specified by hereditary rank tests, including the traces which vanish on the controlling positive element.

Throughout this section, traces on $A$ are the lower semicontinuous extended traces of the preliminaries. In particular, they need not be finite on a dense ideal. If $D\subseteq A$ is hereditary, write $T_{\leq1}(D)$ for the bounded positive tracial functionals on $D$ of norm at most one, and put $$\|z\|_{2,T_{\leq1}(D)}
   =\sup_{\sigma\in T_{\leq1}(D)}\sigma(z^*z)^{1/2}.$$ The supremum is zero if there are no nonzero such functionals. Expressions involving $1-h$ are evaluated in a unitization; a sandwich by an element of $D$ still belongs to $D$.

<a id="stmt-lem-join"></a>
<div id="lem:join" class="lemma">

**Lemma 31** (Joining two matrix cones). *Let $B$ be a $C^*$-algebra, and let $\phi_1,\phi_2:M_k\to B$ be c.p.c. order-zero maps with commuting ranges. Set $h_i=\phi_i(1)$. There is a c.p.c. order-zero map $\phi:M_k\to
C^*(\phi_1(M_k),\phi_2(M_k))$ such that <a id="loc:join-weight"></a>$$\label{loc:join-weight}
 \phi(1)=1-(1-h_1)(1-h_2)=h_1+h_2-h_1h_2.$$ Its weight dominates both $h_1$ and $h_2$. If both original maps commute with a subset of an ambient algebra, the joined map also commutes with that subset.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-join"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-join</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\mathcal C_k=C_0((0,1],M_k)$. Its unitization is the algebra of continuous $M_k$-valued functions on $[0,1]$ whose value at zero is scalar. Realize $\widetilde{\mathcal C}_k\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal C}_k$ as the algebra of continuous $M_k\otimes M_k$-valued functions $F$ on the square satisfying $$F(x,0)\in M_k\otimes1,
 \qquad F(0,y)\in1\otimes M_k.$$ These conditions also make $F(0,0)$ scalar. Here is a direct verification that these conditions characterize the tensor product. The tensor product contains every scalar continuous function on the square, and its evaluation at each point is the entire indicated fiber: use scalar functions vanishing at zero to realize matrix entries on either nonzero coordinate. Given a continuous function satisfying the conditions, choose, at each point, an element of the tensor product having the same value there. Continuity gives a neighborhood on which the error is less than a prescribed tolerance. A finite scalar partition of unity subordinate to these neighborhoods gives an approximation in the tensor product. Closedness proves the assertion.

Let $S$ be the unitary that interchanges the two factors of $\mathbb C^k\otimes\mathbb C^k$, and put $Q=(1-S)/2$. Since $S=S^*=S^{-1}$, $Q$ is a projection, and $$U(t)=\exp(i\pi tQ),\qquad 0\le t\le1,$$ is a unitary path from $1$ to $S$. For $z\in M_k$ define $$F_z(x,y)=
 \begin{cases}
 (x+y-xy)\,U\bigl(y/(x+y)\bigr)(z\otimes1)
                 U\bigl(y/(x+y)\bigr)^*, &(x,y)\ne(0,0),\\
 0,&(x,y)=(0,0).
 \end{cases}$$ At $y=0$ this is $xz\otimes1$, and at $x=0$ it is $y1\otimes z$. At the origin continuity follows from $\|F_z(x,y)\|\le(x+y)\|z\|$; no limit of the unitary itself is needed. Thus $F_z$ belongs to the tensor product. Pointwise it is a positive scalar contraction times a $*$-homomorphism, so $z\mapsto F_z$ is completely positive, contractive, and order zero. Its weight is $(x+y-xy)1$.

The cone homomorphisms associated to $\phi_1$ and $\phi_2$ extend to commuting unital homomorphisms from their unitizations. Nuclearity of the cones lets their product be evaluated on the spatial tensor product. The functions $F_z$ vanish at $(0,0)$. The ideal of functions vanishing there is generated by $\mathcal C_k\otimes1$ and $1\otimes\mathcal C_k$: quotienting by those two ideals leaves only the scalar evaluation at the origin. Its image consequently lies in $C^*(\phi_1(M_k),\phi_2(M_k))$, even when $B$ is nonunital. Evaluation of $F_z$ gives the asserted map and its weight. Finally $\phi(1)-h_1=(1-h_1)h_2\ge0$, and similarly for $h_2$. The last assertion follows from containment of the range in the generated algebra. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-loc-bounded-fill"></a>
<div id="loc:bounded-fill" class="lemma">

<a id="loc:fill-comm"></a>
<a id="loc:fill-oz"></a>
<a id="loc:fill-trace"></a>
**Lemma 32** (Filling bounded traces). *Let $D\subseteq A$ be hereditary, $k\ge2$, $F\subset D$ finite, and $\mathcal P\subset D$ a finite set of positive contractions. For every $\rho>0$ there is a c.p.c. map $\mu:M_k\to D$ such that, for all matrix units $e_{ij},e_{rs}$ and all $x\in F$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \|[\mu(e_{ij}),x]\|_{2,T_{\leq1}(D)}&<\rho,\label{loc:fill-comm}\\
 \|\mu(e_{ij})\mu(e_{rs})-\mu(1)\mu(e_{ij}e_{rs})
       \|_{2,T_{\leq1}(D)}&<\rho,\label{loc:fill-oz}\\
 \sigma\bigl(a^{1/2}(1-\mu(1))a^{1/2}\bigr)&<\rho
       \quad(a\in\mathcal P,\ \sigma\in T_{\leq1}(D)).
       \label{loc:fill-trace}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-loc-bounded-fill"><summary>Proof of stmt-loc-bounded-fill</summary>

*Proof.* The sequences that vanish in the uniform $2$-seminorm define a closed two-sided ideal $J_2$ of $D_\omega$. Indeed the uniform $2$-seminorm is at most the norm, and $\|uz\|_2,\|zu\|_2\le\|u\|\|z\|_2$ for every bounded trace; the triangle inequality and the same inequalities establish ideality and closedness. Write $B=D_\omega/J_2$. Every choice $\sigma_n\in T_{\leq1}(D)$ gives a bounded trace $$\sigma_\omega((z_n))=\lim_{n\to\omega}\sigma_n(z_n)$$ on $D_\omega$, which factors through $B$ by Cauchy–Schwarz. These choices are allowed to depend on $n$.

<a id="loc:fraction"></a>
Fix $0<\delta<1$. Proposition <a href="#prop:bounded-trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:bounded-trace">29</a>, applied at coordinate $n$ with its auxiliary error tending to zero, has the following consequence. Given finitely many positive contractions in $B$ and finitely many elements with which to commute, there is a c.p.c. order-zero map $\psi:M_k\to B$ commuting with those elements and satisfying $$\label{loc:fraction}
 \sigma_\omega(c\psi(1))
 \ge\frac{\delta}{3}\sigma_\omega(c)-C_k\delta^{3/2}$$ for all the chosen targets and all the traces just described. To see the quantifiers, choose positive contractive lifts of the finitely many targets and bounded lifts of the tests, and apply that proposition separately at every coordinate, including real and imaginary parts of the tests, rescaled to contractions, with the requested errors tightened by their fixed norms. The coordinate errors tend to zero uniformly over $T_{\leq1}(D)$. The coordinate maps are order zero, and their commutator sequences belong to $J_2$. This proves the assertion. The lifts exist by completely positive lifting for the finite-dimensional domain when a map, rather than a single element, is being lifted.

<a id="loc:geometric"></a>
Start with joined weight $H_0=0$. Having constructed the preceding joined map, let $r=1-H_j$ and prescribe the targets $r^{1/2}ar^{1/2}$ for $a\in\mathcal P$. Prescribe commutation with the preceding map and with the fixed tests. Apply <a href="#loc:fraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:fraction">[loc:fraction]</a> and join the new map to the preceding one using Lemma <a href="#lem:join" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:join">31</a>. The new weight commutes with $r$, and hence, for any trace $\sigma_\omega$, the defect $$D_j(a)=\sigma_\omega\bigl(a^{1/2}(1-H_j)a^{1/2}\bigr)$$ satisfies $$D_{j+1}(a)\le(1-\delta/3)D_j(a)+C_k\delta^{3/2}.$$ This follows by subtracting $\sigma_\omega(r^{1/2}ar^{1/2}\psi(1))$ from $D_j(a)$; traciality and commutation of $\psi(1)$ with $r$ justify the subtraction without any commutation assumption on $a$. Since $D_0(a)\le1$, induction gives $$\label{loc:geometric}
 D_N(a)\le(1-\delta/3)^N+3C_k\sqrt\delta.$$ Choose $\delta$ so that the second term is less than $\rho/4$, and then a finite $N$ so that the first term is less than $\rho/4$.

Lift the resulting c.p.c. map from $B$ to coordinate c.p.c. maps into $D$. Its exact order-zero and commutation identities in $B$ give <a href="#loc:fill-comm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:fill-comm">[loc:fill-comm]</a>–<a href="#loc:fill-oz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:fill-oz">[loc:fill-oz]</a> on an $\omega$-large set. Its trace estimates also give <a href="#loc:fill-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:fill-trace">[loc:fill-trace]</a> on an $\omega$-large set. Otherwise choose, at every failing coordinate, a violating tracial contraction and one of the finitely many targets; pass to an $\omega$-large set where the target is fixed. Their ultralimit would contradict the strict bound $\rho/2$ from <a href="#loc:geometric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:geometric">[loc:geometric]</a>. Intersect these finitely many good sets and choose a coordinate. This proves all the required uniform estimates. ◻

</details>

We now seek norm-central order-zero maps whose trace deficits against a positive contraction $a$ are arbitrarily small relative to the rank of a fixed $b\in A_+$. Here $a\in\mathop{\mathrm{Ideal}}_A(b)$ and $d_\tau(a)\le C d_\tau(b)$ for every $\tau$ and one finite $C>0$; the precise statement is Proposition <a href="#prop:localized-trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:localized-trace">35</a> below. For positive contractive representatives $(h_n)$ of the weight, the target is $$\tau\bigl(a^{1/2}(1-h_n)a^{1/2}\bigr)\le\eta d_\tau(b)$$ on a common $\omega$-large set for each $\eta>0$, uniformly over traces with $d_\tau(b)<\infty$. To remove the norm defects without losing this estimate, we measure an error by the rank of each positive cut of its modulus. Taking cuts makes this smallness condition stable under norm-null changes of representatives; traces with $d_\tau(b)=0$ will require exact vanishing.

<a id="stmt-loc-relative-ideal"></a>
<div id="loc:relative-ideal" class="definition">

<a id="loc:J-predicate"></a>
**Definition 33**. For $b\in A_+$ define $J_b\subseteq A_\omega$ as follows. An element $z=(z_n)$ belongs to $J_b$ if, for every $s>0$ and $\eta>0$, there is $E\in\omega$ such that $$\label{loc:J-predicate}
 d_\tau((|z_n|-s)_+)\le\eta d_\tau(b)
 \quad\text{for every }n\in E\text{ and every }\tau
          \text{ with }d_\tau(b)<\infty.$$ For $d_\tau(b)=0$ this means exact zero on the left. The set $E$ is common to the entire indicated trace family. It is enough to test positive rational $s,\eta$.

</div>

<a id="stmt-loc-sigma-ideal"></a>
<div id="loc:sigma-ideal" class="lemma">

**Lemma 34** (The relative trace ideal). *Definition <a href="#loc:relative-ideal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:relative-ideal">33</a> is independent of the bounded representative. It defines a closed two-sided ideal of $A_\omega$. Moreover, for every separable $C^*$-subalgebra $C_0\subseteq A_\omega$ there is a positive contraction $e\in J_b\cap C_0'$ such that $ey=y$ for every $y\in J_b\cap C_0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-loc-sigma-ideal"><summary>Proof of stmt-loc-sigma-ideal</summary>

<a id="loc:rank-calculus"></a>
*Proof.* We record the rank facts used in verifying ideality. For $u,v,x\in A$, $$\label{loc:rank-calculus}
 d_\tau(|u^*|)=d_\tau(|u|),\quad
 d_\tau(|xu|),d_\tau(|ux|)\le d_\tau(|u|),\quad
 d_\tau(|u+v|)\le d_\tau(|u|)+d_\tau(|v|).$$ The first follows from Cuntz equivalence of the two squared moduli. For the second use $u^*x^*xu\le\|x\|^2u^*u$, and then the first identity for right multiplication. For the last use $(u+v)^*(u+v)\le2(u^*u+v^*v)$ and the Cuntz inequality for a sum of positive elements.

For $t>0$ the polar cut $$u^{[t]}=u\,q_t(|u|),\qquad
 q_t(\lambda)=
 \begin{cases}(\lambda-t)_+/\lambda,&\lambda>0,\\0,&\lambda=0,
 \end{cases}$$ belongs to $A$, has modulus $(|u|-t)_+$, and satisfies $\|u-u^{[t]}\|\le t$. Consequently a fixed positive cut of a sum or product can be Cuntz compared to the modulus of a sum or product of sufficiently small polar cuts. In detail, choose $t$ so small that the norm difference of the two expressions makes the difference of their moduli less than the desired cut. This is possible uniformly on the bounded sets at issue, by uniform continuity of $z\mapsto|z|$. Norm comparison and <a href="#loc:rank-calculus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:rank-calculus">[loc:rank-calculus]</a> then bound that cut rank by the sum of the ranks of the input polar cuts, or by one such rank for multiplication and adjoints. Apply <a href="#loc:J-predicate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:J-predicate">[loc:J-predicate]</a> with error $\eta/2$ for each of the two summands, and with error $\eta$ for a product or adjoint. Finite intersection of the good sets proves closure under addition, adjoints, and left and right multiplication by bounded sequences. Scalar multiplication follows in the same way.

<a id="loc:representative-cut"></a>
Here are the perturbation details, including the zero-rank case. If $(z_n)$ and $(z'_n)$ represent the same element, then, for a fixed $s>0$, on an $\omega$-large set $\||z'_n|-|z_n|\|<s/2$. Therefore $$\label{loc:representative-cut}
 [ (|z'_n|-s)_+ ]\le[ (|z_n|-s/2)_+ ],$$ by comparing $|z'_n|$ with $(|z_n|-s/2)_+$ in norm, at error strictly less than $s$. This proves representative independence. If $z^{(l)}\in J_b$ converges in the norm of $A_\omega$ to $z$, fix a cut $s>0$ and choose $l$ so that bounded lifts of $z^{(l)}$ and $z$ are close enough on an $\omega$-large set to give <a href="#loc:representative-cut" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:representative-cut">[loc:representative-cut]</a> with $z^{(l)}$ on the right. Apply its predicate at cut $s/2$ and arbitrary error $\eta$. This proves norm closedness. When $d_\tau(b)=0$, every rank comparison in these arguments has an exactly zero right side on the common good set. No assertion about the uncut rank of a norm-small perturbation is being used.

<a id="loc:sigma-stage"></a>
Put $I_0=J_b\cap C_0$. Choose dense sequences $(c_j)$ in the unit ball of $C_0$ and $(y_j)$ in the unit ball of $I_0$. A quasicentral approximate unit for $I_0$ in $C_0$ supplies positive contractions $e^{(l)}\in I_0$ such that, for $j\le l$, $$\|[e^{(l)},c_j]\|<1/l,\qquad
 \|e^{(l)}y_j-y_j\|<1/l.$$ Choose positive contractive lifts $e^{(l)}_n$ and bounded lifts of the $c_j,y_j$. For each $l$, there is an $\omega$-large set $G_l$ on which these finitely many norm estimates hold with $2/l$ in place of $1/l$ and, simultaneously, for $1\le j\le l$, $$\label{loc:sigma-stage}
 d_\tau((e^{(l)}_n-1/j)_+)\le l^{-1}d_\tau(b)
       \quad\text{for all }\tau\text{ with }d_\tau(b)<\infty.$$ Each displayed inequality is one uniform coordinate predicate, so only finitely many predicates are intersected at this stage. In particular, this construction does not enumerate traces.

Replace $G_l$ by the decreasing sets $E_l=\{n\ge l\}\cap\bigcap_{r\le l}G_r$. For $n\in E_1$ let $l(n)$ be the largest $l\le n$ with $n\in E_l$, and set $e_n=e^{(l(n))}_n$; put $e_n=0$ elsewhere. Then $l(n)\to_\omega\infty$. The norm tests imply that $e=(e_n)$ commutes with $C_0$ and absorbs $I_0$. Given $s,\eta>0$, choose $j$ with $1/j<s$ and then require $l(n)\ge j$ and $1/l(n)\le\eta$. Equation <a href="#loc:sigma-stage" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:sigma-stage">[loc:sigma-stage]</a> implies <a href="#loc:J-predicate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:J-predicate">[loc:J-predicate]</a>. For zero-rank traces it continues to assert exact zero at each selected coordinate. Thus $e\in J_b$, completing the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-localized-trace"></a>
<div id="prop:localized-trace" class="proposition">

<a id="loc:relative-rank"></a>
<a id="loc:TL"></a>
**Proposition 35** (Localized norm-central trace filling). *Let $k\ge2$, $b\in A_+$, and let $a\in\mathop{\mathrm{Ideal}}_A(b)$ be a positive contraction. Suppose that, for some finite $C>0$, $$\label{loc:relative-rank}
 d_\tau(a)\le C d_\tau(b)\qquad\text{for every }\tau.$$ There is a c.p.c. order-zero map $\phi:M_k\to A_\omega\cap A'$ with weight $h$ such that every positive contractive representative $(h_n)$ of $h$ has the following property: for every $\eta>0$, on a common $\omega$-large set of coordinates, $$\label{loc:TL}
 \tau\bigl(a^{1/2}(1-h_n)a^{1/2}\bigr)
       \le\eta d_\tau(b)
 \qquad(d_\tau(b)<\infty).$$ In particular the left side is exactly zero when $d_\tau(b)=0$.*

<a id="loc:TL-coordinate"></a>
*The finite-coordinate form permits arbitrary relative trace accuracies together with prescribed finite norm tests. Given a finite set $F\subset A$, finitely many pairs $(a_l,b_l)$ satisfying <a href="#loc:relative-rank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:relative-rank">[loc:relative-rank]</a> with finite constants $C_l$, positive accuracies $\eta_l$, and $\rho>0$, there is a c.p.c. map $\theta:M_k\to A$ whose matrix-unit order-zero defects and commutators with $F$ have norm less than $\rho$, and whose weight satisfies $$\label{loc:TL-coordinate}
 \tau\bigl(a_l^{1/2}(1-\theta(1))a_l^{1/2}\bigr)
     \le\eta_l d_\tau(b_l)
 \qquad(d_\tau(b_l)<\infty)$$ for every $l$, simultaneously over all the indicated traces.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-localized-trace"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-localized-trace</summary>

*Proof.* The relative rank bound will control both the approximate-unit tail in the hereditary construction and the trace mass lost when we remove the ideal $J_b$. Write $I=\mathop{\mathrm{Ideal}}_A(b)$ and choose a sequential positive contractive quasicentral approximate unit $v_i$ for $I$ in $A$. Fix $t_i>0$ tending to zero, and set $$p_i=(v_i-2t_i)_+,\qquad q_i=\chi_i(v_i),$$ where $\chi_i$ is zero on $[0,t_i]$, increases linearly to one on $[t_i,2t_i]$, and is one thereafter. We may assume $2t_i<1$. Then $p_iq_i=p_i$, $\|p_i-v_i\|\le2t_i$, and $(p_i)$ is still a quasicentral approximate unit. Moreover $[q_i]\le[(v_i-t_i/2)_+]$. Compact containment and $v_i\in I$ give an integer $L_i\ge1$ with <a id="loc:hereditary-bound"></a>$$\label{loc:hereditary-bound}
 [q_i]\le L_i[b].$$ No uniform bound on these integers is required.

<a id="loc:trace-norm"></a>
Let $D_i=\mathop{\mathrm{her}}_A(q_i)$. If $d_\tau(b)<\infty$, the restriction of $\tau$ to $D_i$ is a bounded positive tracial functional with $$\label{loc:trace-norm}
 \|\tau|_{D_i}\|=d_\tau(q_i)\le L_i d_\tau(b).$$ Indeed every positive contraction of $D_i$ is supported by $p_{q_i}$, so its trace is at most $d_\tau(q_i)$; an approximate unit obtained from $q_i$ shows equality. Equivalently, use $q_i^{1/m}$ and lower semicontinuity. Finiteness of the bound turns the additive tracial map on positives into a bounded positive linear functional on $D_i$. For $d_\tau(b)=0$ that functional is zero. For a positive finite $d_\tau(b)$, dividing it by $L_i d_\tau(b)$ gives an element of $T_{\leq1}(D_i)$.

<a id="loc:accuracy"></a>
Choose a dense sequence $(x_j)$ in the unit ball of $A$, and apply Lemma <a href="#loc:bounded-fill" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:bounded-fill">32</a> inside $D_i$, with tests $$p_i,\quad q_i x_j q_i\ (j\le i)
 \quad\text{and their real and imaginary parts},$$ and positive target $p_iap_i$. Choose its accuracy $\rho_i>0$ after $L_i$ has been fixed, so that $$\label{loc:accuracy}
 L_i\rho_i\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad L_i\rho_i^2\longrightarrow0;$$ for example $\rho_i=1/(i(1+L_i))$ works. Denote the resulting maps by $\mu_i:M_k\to D_i$, and put $\theta_i(z)=p_i\mu_i(z)p_i$. These are c.p.c. maps into $A$.

<a id="loc:uncorrected-trace"></a>
Their trace defects have the required relative bound already before correction. Put $\lambda_i=\|a^{1/2}(1-p_i^2)a^{1/2}\|$, so $\lambda_i\to0$. The positive element defining $\lambda_i$ is supported by $p_a$; using <a href="#loc:relative-rank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:relative-rank">[loc:relative-rank]</a>, its trace is at most $C\lambda_i d_\tau(b)$. The other part of the defect is, by traciality, $$\tau\bigl((1-\mu_i(1))^{1/2}p_iap_i
                   (1-\mu_i(1))^{1/2}\bigr)
 \le L_i\rho_i d_\tau(b).$$ Thus $$\label{loc:uncorrected-trace}
 \tau\bigl(a^{1/2}(1-\theta_i(1))a^{1/2}\bigr)
 \le(C\lambda_i+L_i\rho_i)d_\tau(b)
 \qquad(d_\tau(b)<\infty).$$ At zero rank both contributions are exactly zero.

Let $\mu,\theta$ be the c.p.c. maps represented by the coordinate maps. Their relevant relations first hold modulo $J_b$. In fact, for each tested $2$-norm defect $z_i$, normalization in <a href="#loc:trace-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:trace-norm">[loc:trace-norm]</a> and functional calculus give $$d_\tau((|z_i|-s)_+)
   \le s^{-2}\tau(z_i^*z_i)
   \le s^{-2}L_i\rho_i^2d_\tau(b).$$ The estimate holds for every fixed $s>0$; at zero rank it holds because all the coordinates at issue belong to $I$ and the restricted trace is zero. Equation <a href="#loc:accuracy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:accuracy">[loc:accuracy]</a> proves that these defect sequences belong to $J_b$. Consequently, modulo $J_b$, the map $\mu$ is order zero and commutes with $p=(p_i)$ and each $(q_i x_jq_i)$. Also $[p_i,q_ix_jq_i]=q_i[p_i,x_j]q_i\to0$ in norm. It follows that $\theta=p\mu(\cdot)p$ is order zero modulo $J_b$ and commutes there with $(q_ix_jq_i)$. It commutes there with the constant $x_j$ as well: using $q_ip_i=p_i$, the difference between the two commutators has norm at most $2\|[p_i,x_j]\|$. For example $p_i\mu_i(z)p_i x_j(1-q_i)
=p_i\mu_i(z)[p_i,x_j](1-q_i)$ for $\|z\|\le1$; the other term is its analogous left-sided expression. Density gives the assertion for all of $A$.

Apply Lemma <a href="#loc:sigma-ideal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="loc:sigma-ideal">34</a> to $C_0=C^*(A,\theta(M_k))$, and let $e$ be its absorbing positive contraction. Define $$\phi(z)=(1-e)\theta(z).$$ Since $e$ commutes with the range, this is c.p.c. Every order-zero polynomial defect of $\theta$ belongs to $J_b\cap C_0$ and is annihilated by $1-e$. Multiplication by $(1-e)^2$ therefore proves the exact order-zero identities for $\phi$. Likewise $[\phi(z),x]=(1-e)[\theta(z),x]=0$ for $x\in A$. Thus $\phi:M_k\to A_\omega\cap A'$ has the required norm relations.

To check the trace loss, the removed weight $j=e\theta(1)$ is a positive contraction in $J_b$. Choose positive contractive representatives $j_i$ and $h_i$ of $j$ and $h=\phi(1)$. Then $\theta_i(1)-h_i-j_i\to_\omega0$ in norm. For $s>0$, functional calculus gives $j_i\le s1+(j_i-s)_+$, whence <a id="loc:correction-cost"></a>$$\label{loc:correction-cost}
 \tau(a^{1/2}j_i a^{1/2})
 \le s d_\tau(a)+d_\tau((j_i-s)_+).$$ Here the second term bounds the trace of a compression of the positive contraction $(j_i-s)_+$, and its rank is unchanged by a positive rescaling if necessary. Given a desired relative error, first choose $s$ so that $sC$ is small, then use <a href="#loc:J-predicate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:J-predicate">[loc:J-predicate]</a> at that fixed cut with a sufficiently small error. Norm-null differences of the lifts contribute at most their norm times $d_\tau(a)\le C d_\tau(b)$. Combining these observations with <a href="#loc:uncorrected-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:uncorrected-trace">[loc:uncorrected-trace]</a> proves <a href="#loc:TL" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:TL">[loc:TL]</a>, uniformly for positive finite $d_\tau(b)$. If $d_\tau(b)=0$, then $d_\tau(a)=0$ and every positive contraction $h_i$ has exactly zero defect against $a$. This also proves the assertion for every choice of positive contractive representative of $h$.

For completeness, the passage to several pairs permits arbitrary finite coordinate tests. Construct the map for the first pair. For the next pair use the preceding construction and reindex its coordinate lifts as follows. At coordinate $n$ the matrix-unit values of all already fixed maps are a finite list of ordinary elements of $A$. Norm centrality of the new map allows an index at which its commutators with that list and with the first $n$ fixed dense tests are less than $1/n$. Intersect this set of indices with the sets imposing its order-zero errors and its uniform trace predicate for its fixed pair, to any desired accuracy tending to zero. A finite intersection is $\omega$-large and hence nonempty. The selected sequence still has <a href="#loc:TL" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:TL">[loc:TL]</a> and now commutes with the earlier maps. Repeat for the finite list of pairs and join.

The joined weight $H$ dominates every original weight $h^{(l)}$. This domination also preserves the uniform trace estimates at coordinates. Indeed choose positive lifts $D^{(l)}_n$ of $H-h^{(l)}$; for any positive contractive lifts $H_n,h^{(l)}_n$, $$H_n\ge h^{(l)}_n-\delta_{l,n}1,
 \qquad \delta_{l,n}\to_\omega0.$$ Their trace-defect difference against $a_l$ is therefore at most $C_l\delta_{l,n}d_\tau(b_l)$ when that rank is finite; at zero rank both defects vanish. Lift the joined c.p.c. map and select one coordinate in the finite intersection imposing all desired norm relations, all prescribed commutators, and all relative trace bounds with strict slack. This gives <a href="#loc:TL-coordinate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="loc:TL-coordinate">[loc:TL-coordinate]</a>. The finite set $F$, and each $\eta_l$, are arbitrary at this final step. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:comparison"></a>
### Adaptive comparison and an unlimited supply of slots

Our goal is to compare a cut of the defect of a central matrix cone with one of its diagonal corners, with a witness bound independent of matrix size. Norm visibility supplies finite coordinate ideal multiplicities $L_n$, which need not be bounded. We fix the norm maps and these integers first, and then choose a tracial cone with relative defect error $\eta_n/L_n$, where $\eta_n\to0$. The ideal comparison bounds the controlling rank by a multiple of $L_n$ times the target rank; passing to a diagonal corner adds only the fixed matrix-size factor. The reciprocal $L_n$ in the trace error cancels this multiplicity. We must therefore obtain an exact coordinate Cuntz inequality before using it. A further spectral margin bounds the witnesses independently of matrix size, allowing the final construction of countably many orthogonal equivalent central slots.

Fix a countable norm-dense family $\mathcal A$ in the positive contractions of $A$, closed under positive rational spectral cuts. Such a family is obtained by starting with a countable dense family and adjoining its iterated rational cuts. Enumerate the tests $$(a_r,\varepsilon_r)\in\mathcal A\times(\mathbb Q\cap(0,1)),
 \qquad r\ge1.$$ We shall use the fixed scalar functions <a id="cmp:cutoff-functions"></a>$$\label{cmp:cutoff-functions}
 f(t)=\min(1,2t),\qquad g(t)=\max(0,2t-1)
       \quad(0\le t\le1).$$ Thus $g$ is nondecreasing, vanishes on $[0,1/2]$, and $g(1)=1$; also $f(t)\ge t$ and $fg=g$. Functional calculus on an order-zero map has the meaning fixed in the preliminaries. The function $f$ enlarges its weight, while $g$ selects a part on which $f$ is one; the enlarged cone will consequently fix the slots cut out by $g$.

<a id="stmt-prop-idealvisibility"></a>
<div id="prop:idealvisibility" class="proposition">

<a id="cmp:I"></a>
**Proposition 36** (Coordinate ideal visibility). *For every fixed $k\ge2$ there is a c.p.c. order-zero map $\nu:M_k\to A_\omega\cap A'$, represented by c.p.c. maps $\nu_n:M_k\to A$, with weights $h_n^\nu=\nu_n(1)$, such that the following holds. For every test $(a,\varepsilon)$, put $$b=(a-\varepsilon/8)_+,\qquad
 y_n^\nu=a^{1/2}g(h_n^\nu)^2a^{1/2}.$$ There are integers $L_{a,\varepsilon,n}\ge1$ and an $\omega$-large set $E_{a,\varepsilon}$ on which $$\label{cmp:I}
 [b]\le L_{a,\varepsilon,n}[(y_n^\nu-\varepsilon/20)_+]
          \quad\text{in }\mathrm{Cu}(A).$$ The integers need not be bounded as $n$ varies.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-idealvisibility"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-idealvisibility</summary>

<a id="cmp:norm-trigger"></a>
*Proof.* First consider finitely many tests. For one of them apply Proposition <a href="#prop:norm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:norm">22</a> with $$x=(a-\varepsilon/16)_+,
 \qquad \gamma=\varepsilon/64.$$ It gives a norm-central order-zero map with positive contractive weight representatives $t_n$ and a constant $0<\kappa\le1$ such that, on a common $\omega$-large set, $$\label{cmp:norm-trigger}
 \|\sigma(x)\|\ge\gamma
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \|\sigma(xt_n)\|\ge\kappa
 \qquad\text{for every irreducible }\sigma.$$ If no representation satisfies the trigger, one can take the zero map and any $0<\kappa\le1$; the assertions below then remain vacuous on the relevant representations. Choose a continuous positive contraction $\chi$ on $[0,1]$, zero at zero and equal to one on $[\kappa/2,1]$, and reparametrize the order-zero map by $\chi$.

Do this for each of the finitely many tests, choosing commuting maps by successive reindexing. Here reindexing preserves the representation quantifier in <a href="#cmp:norm-trigger" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:norm-trigger">[cmp:norm-trigger]</a>. More explicitly, at coordinate $n$, previously chosen matrix-unit lifts form a finite list of elements of $A$. Choose an index of the next map at which its norm commutators with those elements and the first $n$ dense tests are small, its order-zero defects are small, and its own uniform implication <a href="#cmp:norm-trigger" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:norm-trigger">[cmp:norm-trigger]</a> holds. Each condition holds on an $\omega$-large set, and a finite intersection is nonempty. The implication is simultaneous over all representations at the selected index, so it is retained without choosing representations during reindexing. Join the reparametrized maps using Lemma <a href="#lem:join" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:join">31</a>.

<a id="cmp:visibility-implication"></a>
Let $\nu$ denote this finite join for the moment, and choose c.p.c.  coordinate lifts with weights $h_n^\nu$. We claim that, on an $\omega$-large set, simultaneously in all irreducible representations, $$\label{cmp:visibility-implication}
 \|\sigma(a)\|>3\varepsilon/32
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \|\sigma(a^{1/2}g(h_n^\nu)^2a^{1/2})\|>\varepsilon/20.$$ Suppose instead that its failure set belongs to $\omega$, and choose a failing representation $\sigma_n$ at each such coordinate. Intersect with the good set for <a href="#cmp:norm-trigger" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:norm-trigger">[cmp:norm-trigger]</a>. Since $$\|\sigma_n((a-\varepsilon/16)_+)\|
   >3\varepsilon/32-\varepsilon/16=\varepsilon/32>\gamma,$$ that trigger applies, even if the represented norms tend to the boundary $3\varepsilon/32$.

Take the Hilbert space ultraproduct of these representations, and write $\bar a,\bar t,\bar H$ for the resulting positive operators representing $a$, the old unmodified weight for this test, and the joined weight. Norm centrality gives $[\bar a,\bar t]=0$, and <a href="#cmp:norm-trigger" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:norm-trigger">[cmp:norm-trigger]</a> gives $$\| (\bar a-\varepsilon/16)_+\bar t\|\ge\kappa.$$ Consequently the joint spectral part where $\bar a>\varepsilon/16$ and $\bar t>\kappa/2$ is nonzero. Indeed the product is zero where $\bar a\le\varepsilon/16$ and has norm at most $\kappa/2$ where $\bar t\le\kappa/2$. On the indicated nonzero part the reparametrized weight $\chi(\bar t)$ is one. The joined weight dominates this weight and commutes with it and with $\bar a$; being a contraction, it too is one there. It follows that $$\|\bar a^{1/2}g(\bar H)^2\bar a^{1/2}\|
       \ge\varepsilon/16>\varepsilon/20.$$ This contradicts the upper bound at the failing coordinates. Arbitrary positive weight lifts of the joined map give the same limiting operators, so no particular lifting convention was needed for this contradiction. This proves <a href="#cmp:visibility-implication" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:visibility-implication">[cmp:visibility-implication]</a> for each test in the finite group.

For each $l$ perform this construction for the first $l$ tests. Take c.p.c. coordinate lifts of the resulting maps. Choose a decreasing sequence of $\omega$-large sets on which the first $l$ norm centrality tests and matrix-unit order-zero identities hold to accuracy tending to zero, and all the first $l$ implications <a href="#cmp:visibility-implication" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:visibility-implication">[cmp:visibility-implication]</a> hold exactly. Include $\{n\ge l\}$ in the $l$th set, and use its largest admissible stage at coordinate $n$. Lemma <a href="#lem:diagonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:diagonal">19</a> applies, since all these maps and weights are contractions. The diagonal defines a single norm-central order-zero $\nu$, with c.p.c. lifts, and preserves each of the implications on an $\omega$-large set.

At a coordinate where <a href="#cmp:visibility-implication" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:visibility-implication">[cmp:visibility-implication]</a> holds, every irreducible representation annihilating $(y_n^\nu-\varepsilon/20)_+$ annihilates $(a-3\varepsilon/32)_+$. Indeed annihilation of the former is precisely the condition $\|\sigma(y_n^\nu)\|\le\varepsilon/20$, which implies $\|\sigma(a)\|\le3\varepsilon/32$. Primitive ideal separation gives $$(a-3\varepsilon/32)_+\in\mathop{\mathrm{Ideal}}_A((y_n^\nu-\varepsilon/20)_+).$$ The additional positive cut $\varepsilon/8-3\varepsilon/32=\varepsilon/32$ now gives a finite integer $L_{a,\varepsilon,n}$ in <a href="#cmp:I" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:I">[cmp:I]</a>, by the compact-cut ideal comparison from the preliminaries. Choose the integer to be at least one and put it equal to one outside the good set. This makes all constants specified at all coordinates and proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-rankgap"></a>
<div id="prop:rankgap" class="proposition">

<a id="cmp:u-y"></a>
<a id="cmp:bounded-comparison"></a>
**Proposition 37** (An adaptive rank gap). *For every fixed $k\ge2$ there is a c.p.c. order-zero map $\zeta^k:M_k\to A_\omega\cap A'$ with weight $H$ such that, for every test $(a,\varepsilon)$, if $$\label{cmp:u-y}
 u^k(a)=(1-f(H))a,
 \qquad y^k(a)=g(\zeta^k)(e_{11})^2a,$$ there is $r_{a,\varepsilon}^k\in A_\omega$ satisfying $$\label{cmp:bounded-comparison}
 (u^k(a)-\varepsilon)_+
   =(r_{a,\varepsilon}^k)^*y^k(a)r_{a,\varepsilon}^k,
 \qquad \|r_{a,\varepsilon}^k\|^2\le32/\varepsilon.$$ In particular the witness bound is independent of $k$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-rankgap"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-rankgap</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:idealvisibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:idealvisibility">36</a> for this $k$. *Fix its coordinate maps $\nu_n$, their weights, and every integer $L_{a,\varepsilon,n}$ before making any subsequent choices.* They will not be reindexed in this proof.

For a test $(a,\varepsilon)$ retain $b=(a-\varepsilon/8)_+$ and put $d=(a-\varepsilon/4)_+$. Then $d\in\mathop{\mathrm{Ideal}}_A(b)$ and $[d]\le[b]$, so Proposition <a href="#prop:localized-trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:localized-trace">35</a> applies to $(d,b)$ with relative rank constant one. Let $E_r$ be the good sets in <a href="#cmp:I" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:I">[cmp:I]</a> for the enumerated tests. Form decreasing $\omega$-large sets $$G_l=\{n\ge l\}\cap\bigcap_{r\le l}E_r,$$ and let $l(n)$ be the largest $l\le n$ with $n\in G_l$, or zero if there is none. Thus $l(n)\to_\omega\infty$, and all ideal bounds for the first $l(n)$ tests are valid at coordinate $n$. Choose positive numbers $\eta_n\to0$, for example $\eta_n=1/n$.

<a id="cmp:adaptive-trace"></a>
At coordinate $n$ use the finite-coordinate conclusion of Proposition <a href="#prop:localized-trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:localized-trace">35</a> for the first $l(n)$ pairs $(d_r,b_r)$, with the individually prescribed accuracies $$\eta_n/L_{a_r,\varepsilon_r,n}.$$ Include the first $n$ members of a fixed dense family in $A$, and all matrix-unit values $\nu_n(e_{ij})$, in the commutator tests. Ask for norm commutators and norm order-zero defects less than $1/n$. Denote the resulting c.p.c. map by $\theta_n$, and its weight by $h_n^\theta$. Where $l(n)=0$ one may use the zero map. Every finite problem here has already specified its finite integers; its requested trace accuracy is allowed to be arbitrarily small. Consequently the maps represent a norm-central order-zero map $\theta$ commuting with $\nu$, and, for each fixed test, on an $\omega$-large set, $$\label{cmp:adaptive-trace}
 \tau\bigl(d^{1/2}(1-h_n^\theta)d^{1/2}\bigr)
 \le\frac{\eta_n}{L_{a,\varepsilon,n}}d_\tau(b)
       \qquad(d_\tau(b)<\infty).$$ At zero rank this asserts exact zero; alternatively it follows directly from $[d]\le[b]$.

<a id="cmp:positive-weight-lift"></a>
Join $\theta$ and $\nu$ to obtain $\zeta^k$. Use the following specific positive contractive representatives of its weight: $$\label{cmp:positive-weight-lift}
 H_n=1-(1-h_n^\theta)^{1/2}(1-h_n^\nu)
                         (1-h_n^\theta)^{1/2}.$$ They belong to $A$: equivalently, they are $h_n^\theta+(1-h_n^\theta)^{1/2}h_n^\nu(1-h_n^\theta)^{1/2}$. They lie between $h_n^\theta$ and $1$. Since the two weights commute in the ultrapower, they represent the joined weight $H$. For $u^k(a)$ use the positive contractive representatives <a id="cmp:source-lift"></a>$$\label{cmp:source-lift}
 u_n=(1-f(H_n))^{1/2}a(1-f(H_n))^{1/2}.$$ For $y^k(a)$ choose positive contractive representatives $y_n$; for example, if $B_n$ are positive contractive representatives of $g(\zeta^k)(e_{11})$, one can take $y_n=B_naB_n$. All products in <a href="#cmp:u-y" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:u-y">[cmp:u-y]</a> are positive because the matrix map is norm-central.

<a id="cmp:total-weight-domination"></a>
<a id="cmp:ultrapower-cut"></a>
We first convert the norm visibility into an exact coordinate Cuntz inequality. In $A_\omega$ the old weight $h^\nu$ and $H$ commute, $h^\nu\le H$, and both commute with $a$. Monotonicity of $g$ in this commutative functional calculus gives $$\label{cmp:total-weight-domination}
 y^\nu=ag(h^\nu)^2\le ag(H)^2.$$ Write $\varrho$ for the supporting homomorphism of $\zeta^k$. Since $a$ commutes with the range of $\zeta^k$, it also commutes with the supporting matrix units: these are the strong limits of $\zeta^k(e_{ij})(H+t)^{-1}$ as $t\downarrow0$ in the bidual. The right side is the orthogonal sum of $$z_i=ag(H)^2\varrho(e_{ii})\quad(1\le i\le k),
 \qquad z_1=y^k(a).$$ The products are in $A_\omega$. All $z_i$ are Cuntz equivalent, as are their corresponding positive cuts. Indeed $a^{1/2}g(H)\varrho(e_{ij})\in A_\omega$ has its two squared moduli equal to $z_j,z_i$; taking polar cuts gives the same fact for $(z_j-t)_+$ and $(z_i-t)_+$. The two positives in <a href="#cmp:total-weight-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:total-weight-domination">[cmp:total-weight-domination]</a> commute, so scalar functional calculus, splitting a cut of an orthogonal sum, and enlarging its individual cuts give $$\label{cmp:ultrapower-cut}
 [(y^\nu-\varepsilon/22)_+]
       \le k[(y^k(a)-\varepsilon/30)_+]
       \quad\text{in }\mathrm{Cu}(A_\omega).$$

<a id="cmp:exact-coordinate-bound"></a>
Here the cut margins yield an exact inequality before any multiplication by a varying integer. Put $\Delta=\varepsilon/20-\varepsilon/22>0$. Choose one rectangular comparison witness for <a href="#cmp:ultrapower-cut" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:ultrapower-cut">[cmp:ultrapower-cut]</a> in $M_{k,1}(A_\omega)$ with norm error less than $\Delta/2$. Such rectangular witnesses suffice because the source is in one corner and the target is the diagonal of $k$ elements of $A_\omega$. Choose bounded coordinate lifts $X_n$ of this witness. Since functional calculus commutes with the quotient, on an $\omega$-large set, $$\left\|(y_n^\nu-\varepsilon/22)_+
       -X_n^*\operatorname{diag}\bigl((y_n-\varepsilon/30)_+,
                           \ldots,(y_n-\varepsilon/30)_+\bigr)X_n
 \right\|<\Delta.$$ Norm comparison at that coordinate gives $$[(y_n^\nu-\varepsilon/20)_+]
 \le k[(y_n-\varepsilon/30)_+]
 \le k[(y_n-\varepsilon/32)_+].$$ Intersect with the good set for <a href="#cmp:I" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:I">[cmp:I]</a>. Only now multiply the exact Cuntz inequality by $L_n=L_{a,\varepsilon,n}$ to obtain $$\label{cmp:exact-coordinate-bound}
 [b]\le kL_n[(y_n-\varepsilon/32)_+].$$ The bound on $X_n$ may depend on the fixed $k$ and the test. No norm error is multiplied by $L_n$ in this argument.

<a id="cmp:R"></a>
We next prove the coordinate rank gap $$\label{cmp:R}
 d_\tau((u_n-\varepsilon)_+)
       \le\tfrac12 d_\tau((y_n-\varepsilon/32)_+)
       \qquad\text{for every }\tau$$ on an $\omega$-large set. Put $c_n=(1-f(H_n))^{1/2}$ and $v_n=c_ndc_n$. Since $\|a-d\|\le\varepsilon/4$, one has $\|u_n-v_n\|\le\varepsilon/4$. Comparing $u_n$ with $(v_n-\varepsilon/2)_+$ gives $$[(u_n-\varepsilon)_+]\le[(v_n-\varepsilon/2)_+],
 \qquad
 d_\tau((u_n-\varepsilon)_+)\le(2/\varepsilon)\tau(v_n).$$ For the first inequality the norm error is at most $\varepsilon/4+\varepsilon/2<\varepsilon$; the second is the elementary spectral trace bound. From the scalar inequality $f(t)\ge t$ and <a href="#cmp:positive-weight-lift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:positive-weight-lift">[cmp:positive-weight-lift]</a>, $$f(H_n)\ge H_n\ge h_n^\theta.$$ This uses functional calculus on $H_n$ itself and positive domination of $H_n$; it makes no operator-monotonicity assertion about $f$. Traciality and <a href="#cmp:adaptive-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:adaptive-trace">[cmp:adaptive-trace]</a> consequently give, whenever $d_\tau(b)<\infty$, <a id="cmp:source-rank-estimate"></a>$$\label{cmp:source-rank-estimate}
 d_\tau((u_n-\varepsilon)_+)
 \le\frac{2}{\varepsilon}
     \tau\bigl(d^{1/2}(1-f(H_n))d^{1/2}\bigr)
 \le\frac{2\eta_n}{\varepsilon L_n}d_\tau(b).$$

This estimate handles every possible trace value. Set $R_n=d_\tau((y_n-\varepsilon/32)_+)$. If $R_n<\infty$, the exact inequality <a href="#cmp:exact-coordinate-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:exact-coordinate-bound">[cmp:exact-coordinate-bound]</a> gives $d_\tau(b)\le kL_nR_n<\infty$, and therefore $$d_\tau((u_n-\varepsilon)_+)
      \le (2k\eta_n/\varepsilon)R_n.$$ For the fixed $k$ and test, eventually $2k\eta_n/\varepsilon\le1/2$. If $R_n=0$, the same argument first forces $d_\tau(b)=0$ and then gives exactly zero on the left. If $R_n=\infty$, inequality <a href="#cmp:R" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:R">[cmp:R]</a> is automatic in $[0,\infty]$. All good sets used in these alternatives were chosen uniformly over the trace families; thus their intersection proves <a href="#cmp:R" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:R">[cmp:R]</a> simultaneously for every lower semicontinuous extended trace.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:G" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:G">16</a> in $A$ at each coordinate of this common good set. It gives $$(u_n-\varepsilon)_+\precsim (y_n-s)_+,
 \qquad s=\varepsilon/32.$$ Choose $x_n\in A$ with $$\|x_n^*(y_n-s)_+x_n-(u_n-\varepsilon)_+\|<1/n.$$ These witnesses need not themselves be bounded. Define the continuous scalar function $$q_s(t)=
 \begin{cases}\bigl((t-s)_+/t\bigr)^{1/2},&t>0,\\0,&t=0,
 \end{cases}$$ and set $r_n=q_s(y_n)x_n$ on the good set and $r_n=0$ elsewhere. Then $$r_n^*y_nr_n=x_n^*(y_n-s)_+x_n,
 \qquad
 \|r_n\|^2\le s^{-1}\|x_n^*(y_n-s)_+x_n\|
                 \le(32/\varepsilon)(1+1/n).$$ The norm estimate follows from $q_s(t)^2\le s^{-1}(t-s)_+$. Thus these representatives have the fixed bound $\|r_n\|^2\le64/\varepsilon$, and their ultrapower element has norm squared at most $32/\varepsilon$. The compression errors tend to zero along $\omega$, proving <a href="#cmp:bounded-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:bounded-comparison">[cmp:bounded-comparison]</a>. Neither bound depends on $k$ or on any $L_n$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-slots"></a>
<div id="prop:slots" class="proposition">

<a id="cmp:slot-relations"></a>
<a id="cmp:slot-comparison"></a>
**Proposition 38** (Infinitely many central slots). *For every $p\ge2$ there are a c.p.c. order-zero map $\alpha:M_p\to A_\omega\cap A'$, with weight $h=\alpha(1)$, and contractions $s_j\in A_\omega\cap A'$ for $j\ge1$ such that, putting $P=s_1$, one has $$\label{cmp:slot-relations}
 P\ge0,\qquad s_i^*s_j=\delta_{ij}P^2,
 \qquad \alpha(e_{11})s_j=s_j.$$ For every test $(a,\varepsilon)$ there is $r_{a,\varepsilon}\in A_\omega$ with $$\label{cmp:slot-comparison}
 ((1-h)a-\varepsilon)_+
       =r_{a,\varepsilon}^*(P^2a)r_{a,\varepsilon},
 \qquad \|r_{a,\varepsilon}\|^2\le64/\varepsilon.$$ No centrality is asserted or needed for these comparison witnesses.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-slots"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-slots</summary>

*Proof.* For fixed $m\ge1$, apply Proposition <a href="#prop:rankgap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rankgap">37</a> with $k=pm$ and identify $M_{pm}=M_p\otimes M_m$. Define $$\alpha_m(z)=f(\zeta^{pm})(z\otimes1_m),\qquad
 s_j^{(m)}=g(\zeta^{pm})(e_{11}^{(p)}\otimes e_{j1}^{(m)})
       \quad(1\le j\le m),
 \qquad P_m=s_1^{(m)}.$$ These are norm-central contractions, and $P_m$ is positive. The matrix-unit relations for the supporting homomorphism give $$(s_i^{(m)})^*s_j^{(m)}=\delta_{ij}P_m^2.$$ The scalar identity $fg=g$ gives $\alpha_m(e_{11})s_j^{(m)}=s_j^{(m)}$. The weight of $\alpha_m$ is $f(H^{pm})$. Under the indicated identification the first diagonal slot of $M_{pm}$ is $e_{11}^{(p)}\otimes e_{11}^{(m)}$, so Proposition <a href="#prop:rankgap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rankgap">37</a> supplies, for every test, $$((1-\alpha_m(1))a-\varepsilon)_+
       =(r_{a,\varepsilon}^{(m)})^*(P_m^2a)r_{a,\varepsilon}^{(m)}.$$ The witness norm bounds are independent of $m$.

We spell out the diagonalization because the number of slots grows. For every $m$ choose c.p.c. coordinate lifts $\alpha_{m,n}$, contractive lifts $s_{j,m,n}$ for $1\le j\le m$, and positive contractive lifts $P_{m,n}=s_{1,m,n}$. Choose lifts of each comparison witness bounded by $\sqrt{64/\varepsilon}$; the sharper ultrapower bound in Proposition <a href="#prop:rankgap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rankgap">37</a> leaves room for this choice. Put $h_{m,n}=\alpha_{m,n}(1)$. At stage $l$ use $m=l$ and require:

1.  the matrix-unit order-zero identities of $\alpha_{l,n}$, and its commutators with the first $l$ dense tests of $A$, to have error less than $1/l$;

2.  the centrality and the two algebraic relations in <a href="#cmp:slot-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:slot-relations">[cmp:slot-relations]</a> for all slot indices at most $l$ to have error less than $1/l$;

3.  for the first $l$ comparison tests, the equations with positive coordinate expressions $$\bigl((1-h_{l,n})^{1/2}a(1-h_{l,n})^{1/2}-\varepsilon\bigr)_+
           \quad\text{and}\quad
           r_{a,\varepsilon,l,n}^*P_{l,n}aP_{l,n}r_{a,\varepsilon,l,n}$$ to have error less than $1/l$.

For fixed $l$ all these finitely many estimates hold on an $\omega$-large set, because their equations are exact in the ultrapower. The positive coordinate expressions represent the desired products since the weights and slots commute with $A$ in the ultrapower.

Intersect the first $l$ such good sets with $\{n\ge l\}$ to obtain decreasing $E_l\in\omega$, and take $m(n)$ to be the largest $l\le n$ with $n\in E_l$ (zero if none). At coordinate $n$ use the row indexed by $m(n)$. For a slot or witness whose index is larger than $m(n)$, use zero at that coordinate. Every fixed variable is eventually present along $\omega$, since $m(n)\to_\omega\infty$. All maps and slots are contractions, and the witness for a fixed test has its individual bound $\sqrt{64/\varepsilon}$ independently of the row. These are precisely the boundedness hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:diagonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:diagonal">19</a>. The selected maps therefore define a c.p.c. norm-central order-zero $\alpha$, and the selected slots and witnesses define elements with <a href="#cmp:slot-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:slot-relations">[cmp:slot-relations]</a>–<a href="#cmp:slot-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cmp:slot-comparison">[cmp:slot-comparison]</a>. No uniform trace estimate as $m$ grows was needed: each fixed matrix size had already produced exact equations with the stated individual norm bounds. ◻

</details>

<a id="cen:section"></a>
### Simultaneous domination and a central defect filler

The slots of Proposition <a href="#prop:slots" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:slots">38</a> provide a central matrix cone $\alpha$ with weight $h$, a positive contraction $P=s_1$, and orthogonal equivalent slots $s_j$. Put $E=A_\omega$ and $$\Phi(a)=(1-h)a,\qquad \Psi(a)=P^2a\quad(a\in A).$$ We will extend the comparison relations to obtain $\Phi(a)\in\mathop{\mathrm{Ideal}}_E(\Psi(a))$ for every $a\in A_+$. The individual comparison witnesses depend on $a$. To fill the defect centrally, for each prescribed finite $F\subset A$ and $\delta>0$ we need one finite list $v_1,\ldots,v_N\in E$ such that $$\left\|\Phi(a)-\sum_{j=1}^N v_j^*\Psi(a)v_j\right\|<\delta
 \qquad(a\in F).$$ The first part of this section proves this implication for weighted inclusions. Nuclearity allows us to pass from pointwise ideal containment to an inclusion of support-representation kernels; the two tensor-ideal lemmas establish the needed bridge.

The slots then pack the common sum into $z=\sum_j s_jv_j$. These elements need not be uniformly bounded or central. Approximate-unit tests will give bounded compressions with commutators tending to zero, and hence a bounded central element $S$ satisfying $$\alpha(e_{11})S=S,\qquad S^*S a=(1-h)a\quad(a\in A).$$ This defect-filling argument is related to Matui–Sato’s use of excision in small central sequences and property <span class="upright">(SI) <a id="pm-cite-53"></a>[[MatuiSato2012](#ref-MatuiSato2012)]</span> .

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Tensor ideals and support representations

<a id="stmt-cen-elementary-tensor"></a>
<div id="cen:elementary-tensor" class="lemma">

**Lemma 39**. *Let $D$ and $C$ be $C^*$-algebras. Every nonzero closed two-sided ideal of $D\otimes C$ contains a nonzero tensor $a\otimes b$ with $a\in D_+$ and $b\in C_+$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cen-elementary-tensor"><summary>Proof of stmt-cen-elementary-tensor</summary>

*Proof.* Let $0\ne x\in J_+$. There is a pure state $\lambda$ of the unitization $D^{\sim}$ such that $b=(\lambda\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_C)(x)\ne0$. Indeed, product positive functionals detect nonzero positive elements of a spatial tensor product. Fixing the second functional gives a nonzero positive affine function on the state space of $D^{\sim}$, which is nonzero at some extreme point. The resulting pure state does not annihilate $D$. Its GNS representation is therefore nondegenerate on $D$; write $\xi$ for its unit cyclic vector.

Lemma <a href="#norm:excision" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="norm:excision">24</a> gives, on any prescribed finite set of coefficients in $D^{\sim}$, positive contractions $g\in D^{\sim}$ with $$\lambda(g)=1,\qquad
 \|gcg-\lambda(c)g^2\|\longrightarrow0.$$ Here $\pi_\lambda(g)\xi=\xi$, since $g$ is a positive contraction. Choose once and for all a positive contraction $u\in D$ such that $\|\pi_\lambda(u)\xi-\xi\|<1/8$. Approximation of $x$ by finite algebraic sums and excision of their first coefficients show that $$\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}\bigl((ug^2u)\otimes b,J\bigr)\longrightarrow0.$$ The element used to approximate it is $(ug\otimes1)x(gu\otimes1)$, which belongs to $J$; all products with $1$ here are multiplier products. Moreover, $$\|ug^2u\|\ \ge\
 \|\pi_\lambda(g)\pi_\lambda(u)\xi\|^2
 \ > (7/8)^2>1/2.$$ Thus the first factor retains a fixed norm lower bound as the excision accuracy improves.

<a id="cen:product-cut"></a>
We use the following elementary cut observation. If $a,b$ are positive and $\|a\otimes b+J\|<t^2$, then $$\label{cen:product-cut}
 (a-t)_+\otimes(b-t)_+\in J.$$ To see this, represent $(D\otimes C)/J$ faithfully and nondegenerately. The commuting multiplier operators representing $a\otimes1$ and $1\otimes b$ have product of norm less than $t^2$. Their joint spectrum has no point with both coordinates larger than $t$. Joint continuous functional calculus proves <a href="#cen:product-cut" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:product-cut">[cen:product-cut]</a>. Now fix $0<t<\min\{1/2,\|b\|\}$ and choose $g$ so that the preceding distance is less than $t^2$. Both cuts in $(ug^2u-t)_+\otimes(b-t)_+$ are nonzero, and their tensor product belongs to $J$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cen-nuclear-tensor-ideals"></a>
<div id="cen:nuclear-tensor-ideals" class="lemma">

**Lemma 40**. *If $D$ is nuclear and $C$ is arbitrary, every closed two-sided ideal $J\subset D\otimes C$ is generated by the positive elementary tensors that it contains.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cen-nuclear-tensor-ideals"><summary>Proof of stmt-cen-nuclear-tensor-ideals</summary>

*Proof.* Let $J_0$ be the ideal generated by those tensors. If $J_0\ne J$, primitive ideal separation gives a nondegenerate irreducible representation $\pi$ with $\pi(J_0)=0$ and $\pi(J)\ne0$. Let $\pi_D$ and $\pi_C$ be its commuting leg representations, and put $I=\ker\pi_D$, $L=\ker\pi_C$. Nuclearity of $D$ and of its quotient $D/I$ <a id="pm-cite-54"></a>[[CE1977](#ref-CE1977), Corollary 4], together with the quotient identity for the maximal tensor product, gives <a id="cen:tensor-quotient"></a>$$\label{cen:tensor-quotient}
 q:D\otimes C\longrightarrow (D/I)\otimes(C/L),\qquad
 \ker q=\overline{I\otimes C+D\otimes L}.$$ For completeness, the maximal quotient identity follows by the universal property of commuting representations: a representation annihilating the indicated two leg ideals is exactly a pair factoring through $D/I$ and $C/L$. The nuclearity of the first leg identifies both maximal products in this statement with spatial products.

The representation induced by $\pi$ on the target of $q$ is faithful. Here is the needed verification. Write $M=\pi_D(D)''$ and $N=\pi_C(C)''$. The center of $M$ commutes with both legs, hence with the irreducible representation $\pi$; it consists of scalars. Thus $M$ is a factor, and the same holds for $N$. If nonzero positive elements $x\in M$, $y\in N$ had $xy=0$, their support projections $e\in M$, $f\in N\subset M'$ would satisfy $ef=0$. Since $f$ commutes with $M$, it annihilates every $vev^*$, $v\in\mathcal U(M)$. The join of these projections is the central support of $e$, which is $1$ in the factor $M$.
